### Chapter 2.3 — Quantitative Text Analysis: Word Frequencies, Collocations, and N-grams

#### Learning objectives
*   Explain the fundamental principles of quantitative text analysis and its role in revealing textual patterns.
*   Apply methods for calculating and interpreting word frequencies, including the impact of stop words, stemming, and lemmatization.
*   Identify and interpret collocations to uncover significant semantic relationships within a text or corpus.
*   Understand the concept and utility of N-grams (bigrams, trigrams) for analyzing recurring phrases and stylistic patterns.
*   Recognize the limitations and common pitfalls of purely quantitative approaches and the necessity of contextual interpretation.

#### Detailed lesson content
Having established the importance of structured data through text encoding, we can now delve into the core methods of quantitative textual analysis. This approach moves beyond the surface meaning of words to examine their statistical properties, revealing patterns that are often invisible to the human eye but crucial for understanding a text's underlying structure, themes, and even authorship. The quantitative turn in DH is not about reducing texts to numbers, but about using numbers to ask new questions and gain new perspectives on humanistic inquiry.

One of the simplest yet most powerful forms of quantitative analysis is **word frequency analysis**. This involves counting how often each word appears in a text or corpus. While seemingly straightforward, the interpretation requires nuance. For instance, in a novel, common words like "the," "a," "is," and "and" will almost always be the most frequent. These are known as **stop words**, and they typically carry little semantic weight for analytical purposes. Researchers often remove stop words to focus on more content-rich terms. Furthermore, variations of a word (e.g., "run," "running," "ran") might be counted separately, obscuring their shared root meaning. To address this, **stemming** reduces words to their base form (e.g., "run" for all variations), while **lemmatization** reduces them to their dictionary form (e.g., "am," "is," "are" all become "be"). These normalization steps are critical for accurate frequency counts. What do high-frequency content words tell us? They often point to key themes, characters, or concepts. For example, in *Moby Dick*, words like "whale," "sea," "ship," and "Ahab" would predictably rank high, confirming central themes and characters. However, a surprisingly high frequency of a seemingly minor word might signal an overlooked thematic thread.

Moving beyond individual words, **collocations** examine words that frequently appear together. A collocation is a pair or group of words that often co-occur in a text, forming a natural-sounding phrase. For example, in English, "strong tea" is a common collocation, while "powerful tea" is not, even though "strong" and "powerful" are synonyms. Identifying collocations can reveal significant semantic relationships, authorial style, and even cultural nuances embedded in language. If we analyze a corpus of 18th-century political pamphlets, we might find "liberty" frequently collocating with "rights" and "tyranny," highlighting the prevailing political discourse. Collocations are often measured using statistical metrics like Mutual Information (MI) or Log-Likelihood, which quantify how much more often words appear together than would be expected by chance. A common mistake is to simply look for words appearing *near* each other; true collocations imply a stronger, statistically significant bond.

**N-grams** extend the idea of collocations by looking at sequences of N words. A **bigram** is a sequence of two words (e.g., "digital humanities," "textual analysis"), and a **trigram** is a sequence of three words (e.g., "in the desert," "of cold command"). N-grams are powerful for capturing recurring phrases, idiomatic expressions, and even stylistic fingerprints. Analyzing bigrams in a collection of Shakespeare's plays might reveal common poetic structures or rhetorical devices. In a corpus of scientific articles, specific technical phrases would emerge as high-frequency N-grams. These sequences can be particularly useful for tasks like authorship attribution (as authors often have unconscious preferences for certain common phrases) or for tracking the evolution of terminology over time. For instance, tracking the frequency of the bigram "global warming" versus "climate change" in news articles over decades can reveal shifts in public discourse.

Tools like Voyant Tools (an online, web-based suite of text analysis tools) or AntConc (a downloadable corpus analysis toolkit) make these analyses accessible. With Voyant Tools, you can simply paste a text or upload a file, and it will instantly generate word clouds, frequency lists, and collocation graphs. For example, if we input the first chapter of Mary Shelley's *Frankenstein* into Voyant, we'd immediately see high frequencies for "father," "mother," "family," and "Geneva," indicating the strong biographical and geographical grounding of the narrative, even before the monster's creation. We might also see collocations like "natural philosophy" or "deepest gratitude," providing insight into the novel's intellectual and emotional landscape.

However, the interpretation of quantitative results always requires caution. Raw counts, even after normalization, do not inherently explain meaning. They are indicators, not explanations. Decontextualization is a major pitfall: seeing "whale" as the most frequent word in *Moby Dick* is only meaningful if we remember it's a novel about a hunt. Ignoring the narrative, genre, or historical context can lead to superficial or erroneous conclusions. The strength of DTA lies in its ability to *generate questions* for close reading, not to replace it. For example, a high frequency of "light" and "dark" in a poem might prompt a close reading of specific passages to understand their symbolic interplay. Always remember that quantitative methods provide evidence, but humanistic interpretation provides understanding.

#### Key concepts
*   **Quantitative Text Analysis:** The application of statistical and computational methods to analyze textual data, focusing on measurable features like word counts and patterns.
*   **Word Frequency:** The count of how many times each word appears in a text or corpus.
*   **Stop Words:** Common words (e.g., "the," "a," "is") that are often removed from texts before analysis because they carry little unique semantic meaning.
*   **Stemming:** A process of reducing inflected (or sometimes derived) words to their word stem, base, or root form (e.g., "running," "runs," "ran" become "run").
*   **Lemmatization:** The process of grouping together the inflected forms of a word so they can be analyzed as a single item, identifying the word's dictionary form (lemma) (e.g., "am," "is," "are" become "be").
*   **Collocation:** A sequence of two or more words that frequently occur together in a text, often forming a natural-sounding phrase (e.g., "strong tea," "heavy smoker").
*   **N-gram:** A contiguous sequence of 'n' items (words, characters, etc.) from a given sample of text or speech.
*   **Bigram:** An N-gram of two words (e.g., "digital humanities").
*   **Trigram:** An N-gram of three words (e.g., "in the desert").
*   **Voyant Tools:** A web-based suite of text analysis tools for tasks like word frequency, collocation, and visualization.

#### Hands-on activity
**Exploring a Text with Voyant Tools**

1.  Go to [Voyant Tools](https://voyant-tools.org/).
2.  Paste the full text of Edgar Allan Poe's "The Raven" (easily found online) into the text box and click "Reveal."
3.  Explore the default tools:
    *   **Cirrus (Word Cloud):** What are the most prominent words? Do they align with your understanding of the poem?
    *   **Trends:** How do key words (e.g., "nevermore," "raven," "Lenore") appear across the poem?
    *   **Summary:** Look at the word counts. What are the top 10 most frequent words (after stop words)?
    *   **Keywords in Context (KWIC):** Click on a significant word (e.g., "nevermore") in the word cloud. Examine its occurrences in context.
4.  Write a short reflection (200-250 words) on your findings:
    *   What insights did the word frequencies and trends provide about "The Raven" that you might not have immediately noticed through close reading?
    *   Identify one interesting collocation or N-gram that stands out. What might it suggest about the poem's themes or style?
    *   What are the limitations of this initial quantitative analysis for fully understanding the poem's meaning?

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** You are analyzing a corpus of 19th-century American novels. You find that the word "progress" has a very high frequency.
    a.  What initial insight might this suggest about the themes of the period?
    b.  What are two common preprocessing steps (e.g., stop words, stemming, lemmatization) you might apply to "progress" and related words, and why would you apply them?
    c.  What is a potential interpretive pitfall if you only relied on this high frequency count?
    *   **Model Response/Rubric:**
        a.  The high frequency of "progress" suggests that themes of societal advancement, technological development, moral improvement, or historical change were prominent concerns in 19th-century American novels.
        b.  Two preprocessing steps:
            *   **Stop words:** While "progress" itself isn't a stop word, ensuring a comprehensive stop word list is applied to the entire corpus would prevent other high-frequency, low-meaning words from skewing overall analysis and distracting from content-rich terms like "progress."
            *   **Lemmatization (or Stemming):** To ensure all grammatical forms of "progress" (e.g., "progressing," "progressed") are counted as the same root concept. This provides a more accurate count of the underlying idea rather than just specific word forms.
        c.  A potential interpretive pitfall is **decontextualization** or **oversimplification**. A high frequency of "progress" doesn't tell us *how* progress is portrayed (e.g., optimistically, critically, ironically), *by whom*, or *in what contexts*. It might be a positive ideal in one novel and a destructive force in another. Relying solely on the count risks missing the nuanced, complex, or even contradictory meanings attributed to "progress" within the literature, necessitating a return to close reading.

2.  **Question:** Distinguish between a "collocation" and an "N-gram." Provide an example of each and explain how each can offer different types of insights into a text.
    *   **Model Response/Rubric:**
        *   **Collocation:** Refers to words that frequently co-occur in a statistically significant way, often forming natural-sounding phrases, even if not strictly adjacent. Example: "strong tea." Collocations reveal semantic relationships and idiomatic usage within a text, indicating how concepts are naturally paired by authors or within a language.
        *   **N-gram:** A contiguous sequence of 'N' words (or characters). An N-gram of two words is a bigram, three words a trigram. Example: "to be or not to be" (a 6-gram or a sequence of bigrams/trigrams). N-grams capture fixed phrases, common expressions, and stylistic patterns of word ordering. They are particularly useful for analyzing syntax, authorial style, and recurring rhetorical structures. While collocations focus on statistical association, N-grams focus on exact sequential occurrence.

#### AI generation note
Create a 10-minute interactive demo video. Start with a brief animation explaining stop words, stemming, and lemmatization. Then, switch to a screen recording of an instructor using Voyant Tools to analyze a short historical document (e.g., a letter from the US founding fathers). Demonstrate how to generate word clouds, frequency lists, and collocations, explaining the interpretation of each visualization. Use color-coded highlights to point out significant words and their contexts. Include a 2-minute segment on N-grams, showing how to extract and interpret bigrams from the same text. End with a mini-quiz asking learners to identify a potential insight from a given word frequency list. Provide a link to Voyant Tools and a curated list of sample texts for practice.

### Chapter 2.4 — Topic Modeling and Sentiment Analysis

#### Learning objectives
*   Define topic modeling and explain its purpose in discovering latent thematic structures within large text collections.
*   Understand the conceptual basis of Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) as a common topic modeling algorithm.
*   Interpret the output of a topic model, including word distributions per topic and topic distributions per document.
*   Define sentiment analysis and differentiate between lexicon-based and machine learning approaches.
*   Identify practical applications and critical limitations of both topic modeling and sentiment analysis in DH research.

#### Detailed lesson content
As we move beyond simple word counts and collocations, we encounter more sophisticated methods that aim to uncover deeper, latent structures within texts. Topic modeling and sentiment analysis are two such techniques, offering powerful ways to explore thematic content and emotional tone across large corpora. These methods allow humanists to move from "what words are used?" to "what are these texts *about*?" and "what is their emotional valence?"

**Topic modeling** is an unsupervised machine learning technique that discovers abstract "topics" that occur in a collection of documents. Unlike keyword searches, which require you to know what you're looking for, topic modeling helps you discover themes you might not have anticipated. The most widely used algorithm for topic modeling is **Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA)**. Conceptually, LDA operates on two core assumptions:
1.  Every document is a mixture of various topics.
2.  Every topic is a mixture of various words.
The algorithm works by iteratively assigning words to topics and topics to documents, trying to find the best statistical fit that explains the observed word patterns. For example, in a corpus of 18th-century novels, LDA might identify a "topic" characterized by words like "marriage," "fortune," "society," and "gentleman," suggesting a theme of social maneuvering and courtship. Another topic might feature "nature," "sublime," "mountain," and "solitude," pointing to Romantic themes.

Interpreting the output of a topic model requires both statistical understanding and humanistic judgment. A topic is typically represented as a list of its most probable words. For instance, a topic might be presented as: `(0.05) 'king', (0.04) 'crown', (0.03) 'throne', (0.02) 'royal', (0.02) 'power'`. These words, along with their probabilities, help the researcher infer the underlying theme. It's crucial to understand that these "topics" are statistical constructs, not pre-defined categories. The researcher must then apply their domain knowledge to *name* the topic (e.g., "Monarchy and Power"). Visualizations like word clouds for each topic or graphs showing topic prevalence over time or across different authors are common. A significant challenge is determining the optimal number of topics, as too few might merge distinct themes, and too many might create overly granular or incoherent topics. Scholars also look at "topic coherence" scores, which measure how semantically related the top words in a topic are. Common mistakes include treating topics as fixed, mutually exclusive categories rather than probabilistic distributions, or failing to validate the statistical topics with close reading of the documents assigned to them.

**Sentiment analysis**, also known as opinion mining, is another powerful technique that aims to identify and extract subjective information from text, determining the emotional tone or polarity (positive, negative, neutral) of a piece of writing. This can be applied at various levels: word-level, sentence-level, or document-level. There are generally two main approaches:
1.  **Lexicon-based approaches:** These rely on predefined lists (lexicons) of words that are associated with positive, negative, or neutral sentiment, often with assigned scores. For example, "joyful" might have a +2 score, "sad" a -1 score. The sentiment of a text is then calculated by aggregating the scores of its words. Tools like LIWC (Linguistic Inquiry and Word Count) use this method.
2.  **Machine learning approaches:** These involve training a model on a large dataset of texts that have already been manually labeled with their sentiment. The model learns to identify patterns (words, phrases, grammatical structures) associated with different sentiments and can then predict the sentiment of new, unseen texts.

Applications of sentiment analysis in DH are diverse. One might analyze literary reviews to understand critical reception over time, track shifts in public opinion about historical figures in newspaper archives, or even explore the emotional arc of a novel by analyzing sentiment sentence by sentence. For example, analyzing a collection of letters written during wartime might reveal a gradual shift from initial optimism to despair, offering a quantitative lens on historical emotional experiences.

However, sentiment analysis faces significant limitations. It struggles with **polysemy** (words having multiple meanings depending on context), **sarcasm** and irony (where the literal meaning is opposite to the intended sentiment), and **cultural nuances** (what is positive in one culture might be neutral in another). For example, the word "sick" can mean ill or, colloquially, "very good." A lexicon-based approach might misinterpret this. Machine learning models, while more robust, are highly dependent on the quality and representativeness of their training data; a model trained on movie reviews might perform poorly on historical political speeches. The biggest pitfall is taking sentiment scores at face value without critically examining the text and context that generated them. A "negative" score for a literary passage might be part of a character's complex psychological portrayal, not a simple statement of authorial negativity.

Both topic modeling and sentiment analysis offer invaluable "distant reading" perspectives, allowing scholars to process and gain initial insights from vast amounts of text. They are powerful tools for generating hypotheses, identifying trends, and discovering hidden connections, but their results must always be critically interpreted and triangulated with traditional humanistic methods.

#### Key concepts
*   **Topic Modeling:** An unsupervised machine learning technique for discovering abstract "topics" that occur in a collection of documents.
*   **Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA):** The most common algorithm used for topic modeling, which assumes documents are mixtures of topics and topics are mixtures of words.
*   **Topic (in Topic Modeling):** A statistical distribution of words that tend to co-occur, which a human interpreter then labels with a thematic concept.
*   **Sentiment Analysis (Opinion Mining):** The computational identification and extraction of subjective information from text, determining its emotional tone or polarity (positive, negative, neutral).
*   **Lexicon-based Sentiment Analysis:** Relies on predefined lists of words with associated sentiment scores to determine the overall sentiment of a text.
*   **Machine Learning Sentiment Analysis:** Involves training a model on labeled data to learn patterns associated with different sentiments, then applying it to new texts.
*   **Polysemy:** The capacity for a word or phrase to have multiple meanings, which poses a challenge for automated sentiment analysis.
*   **Topic Coherence:** A metric used to evaluate the interpretability of topics generated by a topic model, based on the semantic similarity of the words within a topic.

#### Hands-on activity
**Interpreting Topic Model Output**

Imagine you've run a topic model on a corpus of 100 historical speeches from the American Civil War era, requesting 5 topics. Here are the top 10 words for two of the topics:

**Topic 1:** `union, states, government, constitution, people, nation, federal, law, rights, power`
**Topic 2:** `slavery, negro, freedom, master, emancipation, black, human, justice, rights, condition`

**Instructions:**
1.  Based on the word lists, propose a concise, descriptive name for each topic.
2.  For each topic, identify at least two specific historical or thematic insights that these word distributions suggest about the Civil War era speeches.
3.  Discuss one potential ambiguity or challenge in interpreting these topics. For example, could a word be interpreted differently depending on the speaker's stance?

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** You are analyzing a collection of 18th-century British travelogues using topic modeling. One topic emerges with high-probability words like "savages," "natives," "wilderness," "colony," and "trade."
    a.  What thematic label would you assign to this topic, and why?
    b.  What does the co-occurrence of these words suggest about the perspective or concerns of the travelogue authors?
    c.  What is a critical limitation of interpreting this topic solely based on the word list, and how might you address it?
    *   **Model Response/Rubric:**
        a.  A suitable thematic label could be "Colonial Encounters and Indigenous Peoples" or "Imperial Expansion and Othering." The words clearly point to themes of exploration, colonization, and the perception of non-European cultures.
        b.  The co-occurrence suggests a perspective rooted in colonial discourse, where indigenous populations are often framed in terms of "savagery" or "nativeness" in relation to European "civilization." The inclusion of "colony" and "trade" indicates economic and political motivations behind these encounters. The authors likely viewed these lands and peoples through a lens of resource extraction, territorial expansion, and cultural hierarchy.
        c.  A critical limitation is the **lack of nuance regarding the *tone* or *stance***. While the words indicate the *subject matter*, the word list alone doesn't tell us if the authors are describing these encounters critically, empathetically, or with overt racism. "Savages" could be a neutral descriptor in some historical contexts, or a highly pejorative one. To address this, one would need to perform **close reading** of documents highly associated with this topic, potentially combined with **sentiment analysis** on those specific passages, to understand the authors' attitudes and the specific contexts of these descriptions.

2.  **Question:** Compare and contrast lexicon-based sentiment analysis with machine learning-based sentiment analysis. For what types of DH research questions might each approach be more suitable, and what is a key challenge common to both?
    *   **Model Response/Rubric:**
        *   **Lexicon-based:** Relies on predefined dictionaries of words with positive/negative/neutral scores. Simpler to implement, transparent in its scoring. More suitable for research questions where the emotional language is relatively straightforward and consistent, or for historical texts where a custom lexicon can be built.
        *   **Machine learning-based:** Involves training a model on large, human-labeled datasets. More robust in handling complex language, context, and nuance (e.g., negation, sarcasm). More suitable for contemporary texts or complex literary works where sentiment is subtle or context-dependent.
        *   **Key Challenge Common to Both:** Both approaches struggle with **sarcasm, irony, and polysemy**. A lexicon might misinterpret "sick" as negative when it means "good," and a machine learning model might fail if its training data didn't include enough examples of ironic usage in a specific domain. Both also face challenges with **domain specificity** (a model trained on movie reviews might not work for political speeches) and **cultural context**.

#### AI generation note
Create a 15-minute animated explainer video. Start with a visual metaphor for topic modeling (e.g., sorting a pile of mixed laundry into themed baskets). Then, use a clear, step-by-step animation to conceptually explain LDA, showing documents as mixtures of colored "topics" and topics as mixtures of words. Dedicate 5 minutes to demonstrating how to interpret topic model output using real-world examples (e.g., a topic with words like "war," "peace," "treaty" from a historical newspaper corpus). Transition to sentiment analysis with a visual comparison of lexicon-based (word list with scores) vs. machine learning (training data, model prediction). Use short, contrasting examples (e.g., "This movie was sick!" vs. "I feel sick.") to highlight challenges like polysemy and sarcasm. Include an interactive element where learners match a list of words to a plausible topic name. Provide a downloadable guide on interpreting topic model results.

### Chapter 2.5 — Network Analysis of Texts and Stylometry

#### Learning objectives
*   Understand the fundamental principles of network analysis and its application to textual data in DH.
*   Explain how textual elements (e.g., characters, concepts) can be represented as nodes and edges in a network.
*   Define stylometry and identify its key features for analyzing authorial style.
*   Apply the concept of stylometric analysis to problems of authorship attribution, citing historical examples.
*   Recognize the limitations and complexities of network analysis and stylometry, particularly concerning interpretation and definitive claims.

#### Detailed lesson content
Our journey through digital textual analysis culminates in two advanced methodologies that move beyond individual words and topics to examine relationships and stylistic fingerprints: network analysis and stylometry. These techniques allow humanists to visualize connections within texts and quantitatively assess the unique "voice" of an author, opening up new avenues for understanding social structures, narrative dynamics, and authorship debates.

**Network analysis** in Digital Humanities is a powerful method for visualizing and analyzing relationships within textual data. It borrows concepts from graph theory, representing entities as **nodes** (or vertices) and the connections between them as **edges** (or links). In textual analysis, nodes can represent characters, concepts, places, or even individual words, while edges represent their relationships (e.g., co-occurrence, interaction, shared context). A classic example is the analysis of character networks in novels. For instance, in Victor Hugo's *Les Misérables*, a network analysis might identify characters as nodes and create an edge between two characters if they appear in the same chapter or interact directly. The resulting visualization can reveal central characters (those with many connections), tightly knit groups, and peripheral figures, offering a quantitative perspective on the novel's social structure and narrative focus. The thickness or weight of an edge might represent the frequency or intensity of interaction. Such an analysis can highlight narrative arcs, identify protagonists and antagonists, and even reveal hidden social dynamics that are not immediately obvious from a linear reading.

Beyond character networks, network analysis can be applied to conceptual relationships (e.g., words co-occurring in sentences to form semantic networks), intertextual connections (e.g., documents citing each other), or even the flow of ideas in philosophical texts. Tools like Gephi or Cytoscape allow scholars to visualize these complex networks, calculate metrics like "centrality" (how important a node is in the network), and identify "communities" (clusters of nodes that are more densely connected to each other than to the rest of the network). However, a common pitfall is to assume that a visual representation automatically equates to meaningful insight; the interpretation of network structures always requires careful humanistic contextualization.

**Stylometry**, on the other hand, is the quantitative analysis of literary style. It operates on the premise that every author possesses a unique and often unconscious "stylistic fingerprint" that can be measured through statistical analysis of their writing. This fingerprint is not primarily about vocabulary or plot, but rather about the subtle, habitual choices an author makes in their use of common words, sentence structures, and grammatical patterns. Stylometry often focuses on **function words** (e.g., "the," "a," "is," "and," "of," "but," "for," "with") because these words are used unconsciously and are less subject to conscious stylistic variation or thematic influence than content words. An author's preference for "among" versus "amongst," or their characteristic sentence length, can be highly distinctive.

The primary application of stylometry in DH is **authorship attribution**. This involves using statistical methods to determine the likely author of an anonymous or disputed text by comparing its stylistic features to known works of potential authors. A famous historical example is the analysis of *The Federalist Papers*, a series of essays published anonymously in the 1780s to advocate for the U.S. Constitution. While many were clearly authored by Alexander Hamilton, James Madison, or John Jay, some were of disputed authorship. In the 1960s, statisticians Frederick Mosteller and David Wallace used stylometric analysis (focusing on the frequency of function words) to definitively attribute the disputed papers to James Madison, settling a long-standing historical debate. Another prominent example involves the ongoing debates about Shakespeare's collaborations and the authorship of plays attributed to him.

One of the most widely used statistical methods in stylometry is **Burrows' Delta**, developed by John Burrows. Delta measures the average difference between the frequencies of the most common words in a text and their frequencies in a reference corpus, providing a quantitative measure of stylistic similarity or difference. Texts with lower Delta scores are considered stylistically closer. Tools like the `stylo` package in R make it possible for researchers to perform sophisticated stylometric analyses.

Despite its power, stylometry has limitations. It works best with relatively long texts, as shorter texts may not provide enough data for reliable statistical patterns. Genre can also influence style; an author's legal writing might differ significantly from their poetry, even if both are by the same person. The evolution of an author's style over time, or the impact of collaborative authorship, also complicates analysis. It's crucial to remember that stylometry provides strong statistical evidence, not absolute proof. The results are probabilistic, indicating a high likelihood rather than a definitive "smoking gun." Researchers must always combine stylometric findings with historical, literary, and contextual evidence for robust conclusions.

#### Key concepts
*   **Network Analysis:** A methodology for studying relationships between entities (nodes) represented as a graph, where connections are shown as edges.
*   **Node (or Vertex):** An individual entity or point in a network (e.g., a character, a concept, a word).
*   **Edge (or Link):** A connection or relationship between two nodes in a network (e.g., characters appearing in the same scene, words co-occurring).
*   **Stylometry:** The quantitative analysis of literary style, focusing on measurable features of language to identify unique authorial patterns.
*   **Authorship Attribution:** The application of stylometry to determine the likely author of an anonymous or disputed text.
*   **Function Words:** Common words (e.g., prepositions, conjunctions, articles, pronouns) that primarily serve grammatical functions and are often used as key features in stylometric analysis due to their unconscious usage.
*   **Burrows' Delta:** A widely used statistical method in stylometry that calculates the average difference in the frequencies of common words between a target text and a set of reference texts, indicating stylistic similarity.
*   **The Federalist Papers:** A historical case study where stylometry was used to resolve disputed authorship among Alexander Hamilton, James Madison, and John Jay.

#### Hands-on activity
**Character Co-occurrence Network Sketch**

Read the following very short story excerpt.
```
Alice walked into the bustling tea party. The Mad Hatter greeted her with a wide grin, offering her a cup. Nearby, the March Hare was arguing with the Dormouse about buttered toast. Alice politely declined the tea, preferring to observe the strange company. Later, she saw the Queen of Hearts approach, looking cross. The Queen immediately demanded to know who had eaten her tarts, glaring at the Mad Hatter.
```

**Instructions:**
1.  Identify the main characters (nodes) in this excerpt.
2.  Draw a simple network diagram (you can just list the nodes and edges). Create an edge between two characters if they appear in the same sentence or directly interact.
3.  Based on your diagram, which character appears to be the most "central" in this short interaction? Why?
4.  What kind of questions could a more extensive character network analysis of *Alice in Wonderland* help answer about the novel's structure or character dynamics?

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** Explain the core principle behind stylometry and identify two specific linguistic features that are commonly analyzed in stylometric studies for authorship attribution. Provide a real-world example of how stylometry has been used to resolve a historical authorship dispute.
    *   **Model Response/Rubric:** The core principle of stylometry is that every author possesses a unique, often unconscious, "stylistic fingerprint" that can be quantitatively measured through their habitual use of language. This fingerprint is consistent across their works and distinct from other authors. Two common linguistic features analyzed are:
        *   **Function word frequencies:** The relative frequency of common grammatical words like "the," "a," "is," "and," "but," "of," "with."
        *   **Sentence length distribution:** The characteristic patterns of short, medium, and long sentences an author uses.
        *   **Word length distribution:** The average length of words an author tends to use.
        *   **Vocabulary richness/diversity:** Measures like Type-Token Ratio.
        A real-world example is the attribution of the disputed *Federalist Papers* to James Madison, where stylometric analysis of function word frequencies provided strong evidence.

2.  **Question:** You are analyzing a collection of historical letters and want to understand the social connections between the writers.
    a.  How would you construct a network using network analysis for this purpose, identifying what would be the nodes and what would be the edges?
    b.  What is one specific insight you might gain from analyzing the centrality of nodes in this network?
    c.  What is a limitation of using only letter co-occurrence to infer social connection?
    *   **Model Response/Rubric:**
        a.  **Nodes:** Each individual writer (or recipient) of a letter would be a node.
        *   **Edges:** An edge would be created between two individuals if they exchanged letters (sender to recipient, or vice-versa), or if one person is mentioned in a letter written by another. The edge could be directed (from sender to recipient) or undirected (simply indicating a connection).
        b.  Analyzing the **centrality** of nodes could reveal which individuals were the most influential, well-connected, or acted as key intermediaries in the social network. A highly central node might be a pivotal figure in the historical period, connecting disparate groups or facilitating information flow.
        c.  A limitation of using only letter co-occurrence to infer social connection is that **not all connections are equal**. A single formal letter might create an edge, but it doesn't convey the depth or nature of the relationship (e.g., close friendship vs. professional acquaintance). The absence of a letter doesn't necessarily mean no social connection (they might have met in person). Furthermore, the content of the letters (e.g., tone, topics discussed) is crucial for understanding the *quality* of the connection, which simple co-occurrence does not capture.

#### AI generation note
Create a 12-minute video lecture with dynamic visual elements. Start with an animated explanation of nodes and edges, using a simple example (e.g., a family tree). Then, transition to a compelling visualization of a character network from a well-known novel (e.g., *Les Misérables*), highlighting central characters and community clusters with color coding and zoom effects. Dedicate 5 minutes to explaining stylometry, using a split-screen to show text passages from two different authors and then overlaying a graph comparing their function word frequencies. Feature a brief (2-minute) documentary-style segment on *The Federalist Papers* case study, using archival images and a voiceover. Conclude with a discussion prompt about the ethical implications of authorship attribution. Provide full captions and a downloadable PDF of network analysis and stylometry resources.
