## Module 5: Digital Archiving and Preservation
**Module Goal:** To equip learners with a foundational understanding of the principles, methods, and challenges involved in the digital archiving and long-term preservation of cultural heritage materials and digital scholarship.

### Chapter 5.1 — Introduction to Digital Archiving and Preservation

#### Learning objectives
*   Articulate the fundamental concepts and significance of digital archiving and preservation within Digital Humanities.
*   Distinguish between data archiving and digital preservation, identifying their unique challenges.
*   Explain the historical trajectory of archiving practices from analog to digital, and its implications for DH.
*   Introduce the Open Archival Information System (OAIS) Reference Model as a foundational theoretical framework.

#### Detailed lesson content
Welcome to Module 5, where we delve into the absolutely critical domain of digital archiving and preservation. In the Digital Humanities, our work often involves creating, manipulating, and analyzing digital objects—be they digitized manuscripts, born-digital texts, complex datasets, or interactive installations. Without robust strategies for archiving and preservation, these invaluable scholarly outputs and cultural heritage materials face an existential threat. The digital realm, despite its apparent permanence, is remarkably fragile. Consider the phenomenon of "bit rot," where digital data degrades over time due to storage media decay or software obsolescence, leading to corrupted files that are unreadable. This is not merely a technical glitch; it represents a potential loss of cultural memory, scholarly labor, and historical evidence.

Digital preservation is more than just making a backup copy; it's a complex, ongoing process of managing digital information to ensure its long-term accessibility, usability, and authenticity. While "archiving" often refers to the systematic collection and organization of records for future use, "preservation" specifically addresses the active measures taken to counteract the effects of technological change and decay. For instance, simply saving a document as a Microsoft Word file on a hard drive is archiving. Ensuring that document remains readable, renderable, and understandable in 50 years, regardless of future operating systems or software versions, is preservation. This distinction is crucial for DH practitioners, as our projects often generate unique, irreplaceable digital assets that demand proactive preservation planning from their inception.

Historically, archives have been physical spaces, repositories of paper, parchment, photographs, and artifacts, governed by established practices for conservation, cataloging, and access. The shift to digital has introduced unprecedented challenges and opportunities. While digitization promises wider access and new analytical possibilities, it also brings issues of format obsolescence, software dependency, and the sheer volume of data. Early digital projects, often experimental and resource-limited, sometimes neglected long-term preservation, leading to "digital dark ages" for some pioneering works. For example, early hypertexts or multimedia CD-ROMs, once cutting-edge, are now often inaccessible due to outdated operating systems or proprietary software. This historical context underscores the urgency of integrating preservation planning into every stage of a DH project, moving beyond a "fix it later" mentality.

A foundational theoretical framework for understanding digital preservation is the Open Archival Information System (OAIS) Reference Model, an ISO standard (ISO 14721:2012). OAIS provides a conceptual framework for an archive responsible for preserving information and making it available to a designated community. It defines the functional entities within a digital archive: Ingest (receiving information), Archival Storage (managing and storing information), Data Management (managing descriptive information), Access (making information available), and Administration (overall management). Crucially, OAIS also distinguishes between the Producer (who provides the information), the Management (who oversees the archive), and the Consumer (who retrieves information). The "Information Package" is central to OAIS, comprising both the "Content Information" (the data itself) and "Preservation Description Information" (PDI), which includes provenance, reference, fixity, and context information. This PDI is what allows future users to understand and trust the digital object, ensuring its authenticity and integrity—key concerns in DH scholarship. Without robust PDI, a digital file is just a string of bits; with it, it becomes a verifiable historical record or scholarly artifact. A common mistake is to assume that a digital file's content is self-evident or self-preserving. The OAIS model reminds us that context, provenance, and descriptive metadata are as vital as the data itself for long-term usability and trustworthiness.

#### Key concepts
*   **Digital Archiving:** The systematic collection, organization, and storage of digital information for future use and reference.
*   **Digital Preservation:** The active management of digital information over time to ensure its long-term accessibility, usability, and authenticity, despite technological changes.
*   **Bit Rot:** The gradual degradation of digital data over time, often due to storage media decay or environmental factors, leading to data corruption.
*   **Obsolescence:** The state where hardware, software, or file formats become outdated and no longer supported, making it difficult or impossible to access associated digital content.
*   **Authenticity:** The quality of being genuine and original; in digital preservation, ensuring that a digital object is what it purports to be and has not been altered.
*   **Integrity:** The state of being complete and uncorrupted; in digital preservation, ensuring that a digital object has not been accidentally or maliciously changed since its creation or last modification.
*   **OAIS Reference Model (Open Archival Information System):** An ISO standard (ISO 14721:2012) providing a conceptual framework for an archive responsible for preserving digital information and making it available to a designated community.
*   **Preservation Description Information (PDI):** Metadata within the OAIS model that describes the provenance, reference, fixity, and context of the Content Information, crucial for long-term understanding and trust.

#### Hands-on activity
**Scenario Analysis: The Vanishing Digital Project**
Imagine a Digital Humanities project from 2005 that digitized a collection of rare 19th-century abolitionist newspapers. The project created a custom XML format for text encoding, built a Flash-based interactive interface for browsing, and stored the raw TIFF images on a server that was decommissioned in 2015. The original project team has dispersed.

Your task is to write a short report (300-400 words) analyzing the preservation challenges faced by this project based on the concepts introduced in this chapter (bit rot, obsolescence, authenticity, integrity, OAIS). What specific problems would arise from the choices made, and how might an OAIS-compliant approach have mitigated these issues?

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Multiple Choice Question:**
    Which of the following best describes the primary distinction between "digital archiving" and "digital preservation"?
    a) Digital archiving focuses on creating backups, while digital preservation focuses on deleting old files.
    b) Digital archiving is about short-term storage, while digital preservation is about long-term access and usability.
    c) Digital archiving involves organizing digital files, while digital preservation involves active strategies to ensure files remain accessible and authentic over time.
    d) Digital archiving is for personal files, while digital preservation is exclusively for institutional records.

    **Correct Answer:** c) Digital archiving involves organizing digital files, while digital preservation involves active strategies to ensure files remain accessible and authentic over time.

2.  **Short Essay Question:**
    Explain how the OAIS Reference Model addresses the challenge of ensuring the "authenticity" and "integrity" of digital objects over time. Provide specific examples of what kind of information (PDI) would be crucial for this purpose in a DH project involving digitized historical maps.

    **Model Response Rubric:**
    *   **Excellent (5 points):** Clearly defines authenticity and integrity in the digital context. Explains how OAIS's PDI (Provenance, Reference, Fixity, Context) directly contributes to maintaining these qualities. Provides specific, relevant examples of PDI for digitized historical maps (e.g., scan settings, original map details, checksums, metadata standards, project history). Demonstrates a deep understanding of OAIS and its practical application.
    *   **Good (3-4 points):** Defines authenticity and integrity. Explains generally how OAIS helps, mentioning PDI. Provides some relevant examples for maps, but might lack specificity or depth in connecting them to authenticity/integrity.
    *   **Fair (1-2 points):** Provides vague definitions or explanations. Mentions OAIS or PDI without clear connection to authenticity/integrity. Examples are generic or missing.
    *   **Needs Improvement (0 points):** Does not address the prompt or provides incorrect information.

#### AI generation note
Create a 10-minute animated explainer video that visually differentiates digital archiving from digital preservation using a split-screen comparison. On one side, show a simple "archiving" process (e.g., saving files to a folder), and on the other, illustrate the complex, active steps of "preservation" (e.g., format migration, metadata creation, checksum validation). Dedicate 4 minutes to an animated walkthrough of the OAIS Reference Model, highlighting its functional entities and the components of an Information Package (Content Information + PDI) with clear labels and arrows. Use historical examples of inaccessible early digital projects as cautionary tales. Include a 2-question interactive quiz at the end to check understanding of key terms. Ensure full captions and a downloadable infographic of the OAIS model.

### Chapter 5.2 — File Formats, Metadata, and Interoperability

#### Learning objectives
*   Identify and evaluate various file formats suitable for long-term digital preservation, distinguishing between proprietary and open standards.
*   Explain the critical role of metadata in ensuring the discoverability, usability, and authenticity of digital objects.
*   Analyze different metadata standards (e.g., Dublin Core, MODS, METS, EAD) and their appropriate application in DH projects.
*   Discuss the concept of interoperability and its importance for sustainable digital archives.

#### Detailed lesson content
Building upon our understanding of digital preservation's foundational principles, we now turn to the practical bedrock of any successful archiving effort: file formats and metadata. These are not merely technical specifications; they are fundamental design choices that dictate the longevity and utility of your digital assets. The choice of file format is paramount. While a proprietary format like a `.doc` file might be convenient for creation, its long-term stability is beholden to a single vendor. If that vendor ceases support or changes the format drastically, your data could become inaccessible. This is why preservation best practices strongly advocate for open, non-proprietary, and well-documented formats. For textual data, think plain text (`.txt`), XML, or PDF/A (a specialized archival version of PDF). For images, uncompressed TIFF or JPEG 2000 are often preferred over standard JPEG, which uses lossy compression. Audio often uses WAV or FLAC, and video uses uncompressed AVI or FFV1. The key is to choose formats that are widely adopted, have open specifications, and are unlikely to become obsolete quickly. A common mistake is prioritizing convenience or current software compatibility over long-term access, leading to "format migration" headaches down the line.

However, a perfectly preserved file is useless if no one can find it or understand its context. This is where metadata—"data about data"—becomes indispensable. Metadata provides the descriptive, administrative, and structural information necessary to manage, preserve, and access digital objects. Without robust metadata, a digital image is just pixels; with it, it becomes a photograph of a specific person, taken at a particular time, by a known photographer, part of a collection, and subject to certain usage rights. There are different types of metadata. Descriptive metadata (e.g., title, author, subject) aids discovery. Administrative metadata (e.g., date created, file format, access rights, preservation actions) helps manage the digital object. Structural metadata describes how parts of a digital object are related (e.g., page order in a digitized book). Technical metadata captures details about the hardware and software used in creation or digitization.

Several metadata standards have emerged, each serving specific purposes. **Dublin Core** is a simple, widely used standard with 15 elements (e.g., Title, Creator, Date, Subject) often used for basic resource description and cross-domain interoperability. It's excellent for initial cataloging but often insufficient for complex DH projects. For richer descriptive metadata, especially for textual resources, **MODS (Metadata Object Description Schema)**, an XML-based standard, offers greater granularity and flexibility. When dealing with archival collections, **EAD (Encoded Archival Description)**, also XML-based, is the standard for encoding finding aids, describing the hierarchical relationships within archival collections rather than individual items. For managing complex digital objects composed of multiple files (e.g., a digitized book with images, OCR text, and structural information), **METS (Metadata Encoding and Transmission Standard)** is crucial. METS provides an XML schema for encoding structural, administrative, and descriptive metadata together, acting as a wrapper that binds all components of a digital object and its associated metadata into a single package. Understanding which standard to apply, or how to combine them, is a core skill in DH archiving. For instance, a DH project digitizing a medieval manuscript might use Dublin Core for high-level discovery, MODS for detailed description of the manuscript's content, METS to link the image files to the transcribed text and structural information, and potentially EAD if the manuscript is part of a larger archival collection.

The ultimate goal of using open formats and standardized metadata is to achieve interoperability. Interoperability means that different systems, organizations, and communities can exchange and make use of information seamlessly. In the context of digital archives, it means that data ingested into one repository can be understood and potentially migrated to another, that search engines can harvest metadata from various sources, and that researchers can combine datasets from disparate collections. Without interoperability, digital archives risk becoming isolated silos, hindering scholarly collaboration and the aggregation of knowledge. The challenge often lies in the "semantic interoperability"—ensuring that not just the data can be exchanged, but that its meaning and context are also preserved across different systems and communities. This often requires the use of controlled vocabularies, ontologies, and shared data models, which we touched upon in our module on Data Modeling. A common pitfall here is the "not invented here" syndrome, where projects develop idiosyncratic metadata schemes that, while perfectly logical internally, hinder broader sharing and reuse. Adhering to established standards, even with their perceived limitations, is almost always the more sustainable path.

#### Key concepts
*   **File Format:** The specific structure in which digital data is encoded and stored (e.g., TIFF, JPEG, XML, PDF/A).
*   **Proprietary Format:** A file format whose specifications are controlled by a specific company or organization, often requiring specific software for access (e.g., .doc, .psd).
*   **Open Standard:** A file format or protocol whose specifications are publicly available and developed through a collaborative, transparent process, promoting interoperability and long-term access.
*   **Metadata:** Structured information that describes, explains, locates, or otherwise makes it easier to retrieve, use, or manage an information resource.
*   **Descriptive Metadata:** Information that describes a resource for purposes of discovery and identification (e.g., title, author, subject).
*   **Administrative Metadata:** Information that manages a resource, such as when and how it was created, file type, and access restrictions.
*   **Structural Metadata:** Information about the relationships between parts of a resource (e.g., page order in a digitized book).
*   **Technical Metadata:** Information about the technical characteristics of a digital file, such as resolution, color depth, or sampling rate.
*   **Dublin Core:** A simple, widely used metadata standard with 15 elements for basic resource description.
*   **MODS (Metadata Object Description Schema):** An XML-based schema for rich descriptive metadata, offering more granularity than Dublin Core.
*   **EAD (Encoded Archival Description):** An XML-based standard for encoding finding aids, describing the hierarchical structure and content of archival collections.
*   **METS (Metadata Encoding and Transmission Standard):** An XML schema for encoding descriptive, administrative, and structural metadata for complex digital objects, binding multiple files into a single package.
*   **Interoperability:** The ability of different information systems, devices, or applications to connect, communicate, and exchange data in a coordinated and seamless manner.

#### Hands-on activity
**Metadata Schema Design for a DH Project**
You are designing a DH project to digitize and analyze a collection of early 20th-century political cartoons. The collection includes high-resolution image files, transcriptions of accompanying text, and scholarly annotations.

Your task is to propose a metadata strategy by:
1.  Identifying at least three different metadata standards (from Dublin Core, MODS, METS, EAD) that would be appropriate for this project.
2.  For each chosen standard, explain *why* it is suitable and provide 2-3 specific metadata elements you would use from that standard, along with an example value for a hypothetical cartoon titled "The March of Progress" by artist "A. Cartoonist," published in "The Daily Tribune" on "1905-03-15."
3.  Briefly describe how these standards would work together to ensure comprehensive description and long-term preservation.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Multiple Choice Question:**
    A Digital Humanities project involves a complex digital object consisting of high-resolution images of a medieval manuscript, OCR-generated text for each page, and scholarly annotations linking specific words to historical commentaries. Which metadata standard would be most appropriate for encoding the structural relationships between these different components into a single package?
    a) Dublin Core
    b) EAD
    c) MODS
    d) METS

    **Correct Answer:** d) METS

2.  **Short Answer Question:**
    You are advising a small historical society on preserving their collection of born-digital newsletters (originally created in Microsoft Publisher and Word). What specific advice would you give them regarding file formats and why, considering long-term preservation and interoperability? What common mistake should they avoid?

    **Model Response Rubric:**
    *   **Excellent (5 points):** Identifies proprietary formats (Publisher, Word) as a preservation risk. Recommends migration to open, non-proprietary formats like PDF/A for documents, or plain text/XML for content extraction. Explains *why* these formats are better (open specifications, wider adoption, software independence). Clearly identifies the common mistake of relying on proprietary formats or not planning for migration.
    *   **Good (3-4 points):** Identifies proprietary formats as an issue and suggests open alternatives. Provides a basic reason for the choice. May not fully articulate the "why" or explicitly name the common mistake.
    *   **Fair (1-2 points):** Suggests some alternative formats but doesn't clearly explain the proprietary vs. open distinction or the rationale. Misses the common mistake.
    *   **Needs Improvement (0 points):** Provides incorrect or irrelevant advice.

#### AI generation note
Produce a 12-minute interactive module with a split-screen interface. On the left, display examples of various file formats (e.g., a TIFF image, an XML document, a PDF/A file) and their code/structure snippets. On the right, feature a live instructor explaining the pros and cons of each for preservation. Dedicate 5 minutes to a guided walkthrough of metadata examples: show a Dublin Core record for a book, then a MODS record for the same book highlighting richer detail, then a METS structure diagram showing how images and text are linked. Include a drag-and-drop exercise where learners match metadata elements to their type (descriptive, administrative, structural). Use clear visual cues and color-coding for different metadata standards. Provide a downloadable cheat sheet of recommended preservation formats and common metadata elements.

### Chapter 5.3 — Digital Curation and Repository Systems

#### Learning objectives
*   Define digital curation and explain its lifecycle approach to managing digital assets.
*   Differentiate between various types of digital repositories (institutional, disciplinary, trusted) and their respective roles.
*   Evaluate common open-source repository software platforms (e.g., DSpace, Fedora, Islandora, Omeka) for DH project needs.
*   Design a basic conceptual framework for a digital repository tailored to a specific DH project scenario.

#### Detailed lesson content
Having established the importance of file formats and metadata, we now consider the broader framework within which these elements are managed: digital curation and repository systems. Digital curation is an active, ongoing process of managing, preserving, and adding value to digital data throughout its lifecycle. It extends beyond mere preservation to encompass activities like data selection, appraisal, organization, description, maintenance, and eventual disposal or reuse. Think of it as the continuous care and feeding of digital assets, ensuring not just their survival, but their continued utility and interpretability for future generations of scholars and the public. The Digital Curation Centre (DCC) provides a widely recognized "DCC Lifecycle Model" which illustrates this iterative process, emphasizing actions like conceptualization, creation, appraisal, ingest, preservation action, storage, access, and transformation, with ongoing review and re-appraisal. This lifecycle approach highlights that curation is not a one-time event but a continuous commitment, especially crucial for dynamic DH projects that evolve over time.

At the heart of digital curation are digital repositories—systems designed to store, manage, and provide access to digital objects and their associated metadata. These are not just glorified hard drives; they are sophisticated infrastructures built to implement preservation strategies. We can categorize repositories in several ways. **Institutional repositories** are typically maintained by universities or research institutions to collect, preserve, and disseminate the scholarly output of their faculty and researchers (e.g., theses, articles, datasets, DH projects). **Disciplinary repositories** focus on specific academic fields, aggregating data and publications relevant to that discipline, often fostering community standards and sharing (e.g., Zenodo for scientific data, HathiTrust for digitized books). Beyond these, the concept of a **trusted digital repository** is paramount. A trusted repository adheres to established standards and best practices for long-term preservation, often undergoing rigorous audits and certifications (like the CoreTrustSeal). This certification provides assurance that the repository is committed to the long-term preservation of its holdings, offering a crucial layer of confidence for DH projects seeking to ensure their work endures. For a DH project, choosing where to deposit your data or even building your own repository requires careful consideration of these distinctions.

For DH practitioners, understanding the landscape of repository software is essential. Many open-source platforms are available, each with strengths and weaknesses. **DSpace** is a popular, robust institutional repository software, excellent for managing diverse digital content types and providing stable, long-term access. It's highly configurable and supports various metadata standards. **Fedora (Flexible Extensible Digital Object Repository Architecture)** is a more flexible, object-oriented repository system, often used as a backend for more complex digital library applications. It's particularly strong for managing complex digital objects with multiple components and relationships, making it suitable for intricate DH projects. **Islandora** builds on Fedora, integrating it with Drupal (a content management system) to provide a rich user interface and extensive functionality for digital libraries, archives, and museums. It's often favored for its ability to present complex digital collections in an engaging way. Finally, **Omeka** is a lightweight, user-friendly web publishing platform specifically designed for displaying collections and creating online exhibitions. While not a full-fledged preservation repository in the same vein as DSpace or Fedora, Omeka is incredibly popular in DH for its ease of use in creating public-facing project sites and exhibitions, often serving as the "access layer" for content preserved in a more robust backend system. The choice among these depends heavily on the project's scale, complexity, technical expertise, and primary goals (e.g., long-term preservation vs. public exhibition). A common mistake is to confuse a public-facing exhibition platform like Omeka with a comprehensive preservation repository; while Omeka is excellent for access, it typically needs to be paired with a dedicated preservation solution for true long-term viability.

Designing a digital repository for a DH project involves more than just picking software; it requires a strategic approach. Consider a project creating a digital archive of oral histories from a specific community. The repository would need to handle audio files (WAV, FLAC), transcripts (XML, PDF/A), metadata (MODS, Dublin Core), and potentially associated images or documents. You'd need to plan for ingest workflows (how new interviews are added), storage solutions (local server, cloud, or a combination), access policies (who can listen, download, transcribe), and preservation actions (format migration plans). The conceptual framework would involve defining the "designated community" (who are the intended users?), the "information package" (what constitutes a complete oral history record?), and the "preservation policies" (how long will it be kept, what formats are acceptable?). This design process requires collaboration between DH scholars, archivists, IT professionals, and community stakeholders, ensuring that the repository serves both scholarly and community needs while adhering to preservation best practices.

#### Key concepts
*   **Digital Curation:** The active management of digital data and assets throughout their entire lifecycle, from creation to preservation and reuse, to ensure their long-term value and accessibility.
*   **DCC Lifecycle Model:** A widely recognized model developed by the Digital Curation Centre, illustrating the iterative stages and activities involved in digital curation.
*   **Digital Repository:** A system designed to store, manage, preserve, and provide access to digital objects and their associated metadata.
*   **Institutional Repository:** A digital archive maintained by an academic institution to collect, preserve, and disseminate the scholarly output of its faculty, staff, and students.
*   **Disciplinary Repository:** A digital archive focused on a specific academic field, aggregating data and publications relevant to that discipline.
*   **Trusted Digital Repository:** A digital repository that adheres to established standards and best practices for long-term preservation, often undergoing certification (e.g., CoreTrustSeal) to provide assurance of its capabilities.
*   **DSpace:** A popular open-source institutional repository software for managing diverse digital content types.
*   **Fedora (Flexible Extensible Digital Object Repository Architecture):** A flexible, object-oriented repository system often used as a backend for complex digital library applications.
*   **Islandora:** A digital asset management system that integrates Fedora, Drupal, and Solr to provide a rich platform for digital libraries and archives.
*   **Omeka:** A user-friendly web publishing platform designed for displaying collections and creating online exhibitions, often used as a public-facing access layer for DH projects.

#### Hands-on activity
**Repository Selection for a DH Project**
You are leading a Digital Humanities project that aims to create a comprehensive digital archive of historical political pamphlets from the American Revolution. The project will involve:
*   Digitized images of pamphlets (high-res TIFFs).
*   OCR-generated full text for each pamphlet.
*   Scholarly annotations and XML-encoded TEI versions of selected pamphlets.
*   A public-facing exhibition website with interactive timelines and maps.
*   Long-term preservation of all digital assets.

Based on the repository systems discussed (DSpace, Fedora, Islandora, Omeka), propose a repository solution. Explain which platform(s) you would choose and *why*, considering the project's specific needs for preservation, complex object management, and public access. If you propose a combination, explain how they would integrate.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **True/False Question:**
    A digital repository built using Omeka alone is generally considered a fully trusted digital preservation solution for long-term archival needs.

    **Correct Answer:** False. While Omeka is excellent for public access and exhibition, it typically needs to be paired with a more robust, dedicated preservation repository (like DSpace or Fedora) for true long-term archival security and adherence to trusted digital repository standards.

2.  **Short Essay Question:**
    Describe the "lifecycle approach" to digital curation, referencing the DCC Lifecycle Model. How does this approach differ from simply "backing up" data, and why is it particularly relevant for dynamic, evolving Digital Humanities projects?

    **Model Response Rubric:**
    *   **Excellent (5 points):** Clearly explains the DCC Lifecycle Model as an iterative, continuous process involving stages like creation, appraisal, ingest, preservation actions, access, and re-appraisal. Explicitly contrasts this with simple data backup by emphasizing active management, value addition, and long-term planning. Connects the continuous nature of curation to the evolving needs and outputs of DH projects, highlighting how it ensures ongoing usability and interpretability.
    *   **Good (3-4 points):** Describes several stages of the DCC Lifecycle Model. Differentiates curation from backup, but perhaps less clearly on the "active management" aspect. Makes a general connection to DH projects.
    *   **Fair (1-2 points):** Provides a partial list of lifecycle stages. Mentions backup but doesn't fully articulate the difference. Connection to DH projects is weak.
    *   **Needs Improvement (0 points):** Fails to describe the lifecycle or differentiate from backup, or provides incorrect information.

#### AI generation note
Develop an 11-minute animated explainer video focusing on the digital curation lifecycle and repository systems. Begin with a visual representation of the DCC Lifecycle Model, animating each stage with brief descriptions and examples relevant to a DH project (e.g., "Creation: TEI encoding," "Preservation Action: Format Migration"). Then, dedicate 5 minutes to a virtual tour of different repository interfaces: show screenshots/mockups of DSpace (institutional focus), Fedora (complex object backend), Islandora (integrated Drupal interface), and Omeka (exhibition site), highlighting their distinct features and use cases. Use a comparative chart to summarize their strengths for different DH needs. Include a reflection prompt asking learners to consider which repository type best suits their own research interests. Provide full captions and a downloadable comparison chart of repository platforms.

### Chapter 5.4 — Legal, Ethical, and Access Considerations in Digital Archives

#### Learning objectives
*   Analyze the complexities of copyright, intellectual property, and fair use in the context of digital archiving and DH projects.
*   Identify key privacy and data protection regulations (e.g., GDPR, CCPA) and their implications for handling sensitive digital data.
*   Discuss ethical considerations related to representation, bias, and decolonization within digital archives.
*   Evaluate different models of access and digital rights management for preserved digital content.

#### Detailed lesson content
As Digital Humanists, our work with digital archives and cultural heritage materials is not purely technical; it is deeply intertwined with legal, ethical, and social responsibilities. Navigating these considerations is paramount to ensuring our projects are not only preserved but also just, equitable, and compliant. One of the most pervasive challenges is **copyright and intellectual property (IP)**. When digitizing materials, even if they are physically held in an archive, the digital copies themselves can be subject to new IP rights, and the underlying works may still be protected. Understanding the duration of copyright (life of the author plus 70 years in many jurisdictions), public domain status, and the concept of **fair use** (or fair dealing in other legal systems) is crucial. Fair use allows for limited use of copyrighted material without permission for purposes such as criticism, commentary, news reporting, teaching, scholarship, or research. However, fair use is a legal defense, not an absolute right, and its application can be ambiguous, especially in a global digital environment. For DH projects, this means careful rights clearance, seeking permission where necessary, and clearly documenting the copyright status of all digitized and born-digital content. A common pitfall is assuming that anything found online or in a library is free to use; always verify copyright status.

Beyond copyright, **privacy and data protection** have become increasingly critical, especially when DH projects involve personal data, even if historical. Regulations like the European Union's **General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR)** and the California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA) set strict rules about how personal data is collected, stored, processed, and shared. For DH, this impacts projects dealing with oral histories, social media data, personal correspondence, or any material that could identify living individuals. Anonymization, pseudonymization, and secure data handling practices are essential. For instance, an oral history project might need explicit consent from interviewees for digital archiving and public access, specifying how their data will be used and protected. Failure to comply can lead to significant legal penalties and ethical breaches. This also extends to the privacy of historical figures; while their personal data might not fall under modern privacy laws, ethical considerations regarding their reputation and legacy remain.

Ethical considerations extend beyond legal compliance to encompass broader issues of **representation, bias, and decolonization** within digital archives. Traditional archives often reflect the biases of their creators and collectors, privileging certain narratives, voices, and cultures while marginalizing others. When these archives are digitized, these biases can be amplified and perpetuated in the digital realm. Digital Humanists have a responsibility to critically examine the provenance and content of the materials they digitize and curate. This might involve actively seeking out underrepresented voices, critically annotating existing collections to highlight biases, or even challenging the very frameworks of categorization and description. For example, a project digitizing colonial-era documents might need to include critical contextual metadata that acknowledges the power dynamics inherent in the original records, rather than simply reproducing them without comment. The movement towards "decolonizing the archive" in DH calls for a radical re-evaluation of archival practices, aiming to dismantle colonial power structures embedded in collections and promote more inclusive, equitable representations of history and culture. This is not just about adding more diverse content, but about fundamentally rethinking how archives are built, described, and accessed.

Finally, the question of **access** to digital archives is central. While digital preservation aims to ensure long-term availability, the terms of that availability can vary widely. **Open access** promotes the free, immediate, online availability of research outputs, often under licenses like Creative Commons, maximizing dissemination and reuse. This is a powerful model for many DH projects, aligning with scholarly ideals of knowledge sharing. However, not all content can or should be open access. Some materials may require **restricted access** due to copyright, privacy concerns, cultural sensitivities, or donor agreements. This might involve access only within a specific institutional network, to authenticated researchers, or after an embargo period. **Digital Rights Management (DRM)** technologies can be employed to control access and use, though they are often controversial due to their potential to restrict legitimate fair use or hinder long-term preservation efforts. Balancing the desire for broad access with legal and ethical obligations is a constant negotiation in digital archiving. A practical scenario might involve a DH project digitizing Indigenous cultural heritage materials; here, access might be restricted to community members, or specific protocols might be established in consultation with the community to ensure culturally appropriate use, demonstrating that "open" is not always the most ethical or responsible path.

#### Key concepts
*   **Copyright:** The exclusive legal right, given to an originator or an assignee to print, publish, perform, film, or record literary, artistic, or musical material, and to authorize others to do the same.
*   **Intellectual Property (IP):** Creations of the mind, such as inventions; literary and artistic works; designs; and symbols, names and images used in commerce.
*   **Public Domain:** Works whose intellectual property rights have expired, been forfeited, or are inapplicable, making them available for public use without restriction.
*   **Fair Use (or Fair Dealing):** A legal doctrine that permits limited use of copyrighted material without acquiring permission from the rights holders, for purposes such as criticism, commentary, news reporting, teaching, scholarship, or research.
*   **Privacy:** The right of individuals to control the collection, use, and disclosure of their personal information.
*   **Data Protection:** Legal frameworks and practices designed to safeguard personal data from unauthorized access, use, or disclosure.
*   **GDPR (General Data Protection Regulation):** A regulation in EU law on data protection and privacy in the European Union and the European Economic Area.
*   **CCPA (California Consumer Privacy Act):** A state statute intended to enhance privacy rights and consumer protection for residents of California, United States.
*   **Decolonizing the Archive:** A critical approach to archival practice that seeks to challenge and dismantle colonial power structures, biases, and narratives embedded within archival collections, promoting more inclusive and equitable representation.
*   **Open Access:** The practice of providing free, immediate, online access to scholarly research, often with rights to reuse, typically under Creative Commons licenses.
*   **Restricted Access:** Limiting access to digital content to specific individuals, groups, or under certain conditions due to legal, ethical, or cultural reasons.
*   **Digital Rights Management (DRM):** Technologies used by copyright holders to control the use, modification, and distribution of copyrighted works, often controversial in preservation contexts.

#### Hands-on activity
**Ethical Dilemma: The Sensitive Digital Collection**
You are working on a DH project to digitize and make accessible a collection of personal letters and diaries from a prominent, but controversial, historical figure. The collection contains highly personal reflections, some of which could be considered offensive by modern standards, and details about living descendants who have not given explicit permission for public access.

Write a short memo (300-400 words) outlining the legal and ethical considerations you would need to address before making this collection publicly available. Specifically, discuss:
1.  Copyright status of the letters/diaries.
2.  Privacy concerns for the historical figure and living descendants.
3.  Ethical responsibilities regarding potentially offensive content and how you might address issues of representation or bias.
4.  Proposed access model (e.g., open, restricted, embargoed) and justification.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Multiple Choice Question:**
    A DH project digitizes a collection of 19th-century photographs from a local historical society. The society wants to make them available online for public use and research. Which of the following is the *most likely* copyright status for these photographs, assuming the photographer died in 1890?
    a) Still under copyright, requiring permission from the photographer's descendants.
    b) Public domain, free for unrestricted use.
    c) Subject to GDPR regulations due to personal images.
    d) Protected by Digital Rights Management (DRM).

    **Correct Answer:** b) Public domain, free for unrestricted use (assuming standard copyright terms of life+70 years have expired).

2.  **Short Essay Question:**
    Discuss how the principle of "decolonizing the archive" might influence the metadata creation process for a DH project digitizing historical records from a colonial administration. Provide at least two concrete examples of how traditional metadata might be challenged or augmented to promote more equitable representation.

    **Model Response Rubric:**
    *   **Excellent (5 points):** Clearly defines "decolonizing the archive" as challenging power structures and biases. Explains how this influences metadata by moving beyond mere reproduction of original archival descriptions. Provides two strong, concrete examples: e.g., adding critical contextual notes to original titles/descriptions that use problematic language; creating new subject headings/keywords that reflect Indigenous perspectives or counter-narratives; linking to community-generated knowledge or oral histories; documenting the provenance of bias in the original record.
    *   **Good (3-4 points):** Defines decolonizing the archive generally. Explains its relevance to metadata. Provides one or two examples, but they might be less specific or lack depth in showing how traditional metadata is challenged.
    *   **Fair (1-2 points):** Provides a vague definition or explanation of decolonizing the archive. Examples are generic or missing a clear link to metadata.
    *   **Needs Improvement (0 points):** Does not address the prompt or provides incorrect information.

#### AI generation note
Create a 10-minute animated infographic video that visually breaks down complex legal and ethical concepts. Start with a segment on copyright, using a timeline to illustrate public domain vs. copyrighted works and simple scenarios for fair use. Transition to data privacy, using a "data flow" animation to show how personal data moves through a DH project, highlighting GDPR/CCPA compliance points (e.g., consent forms, anonymization). Dedicate 3 minutes to a case study on "decolonizing metadata," showing a problematic historical archival description and then demonstrating how it could be augmented with critical annotations or alternative subject headings. End with a discussion prompt on balancing open access with cultural sensitivity. Include full captions and a downloadable checklist for legal/ethical review in DH projects.

### Chapter 5.5 — Preservation Strategies and Technologies

#### Learning objectives
*   Evaluate various digital preservation strategies (e.g., migration, emulation, normalization) and their suitability for different types of digital objects.
*   Compare and contrast cloud-based versus local storage solutions for digital archives, considering cost, security, and scalability.
*   Explain the role of digital forensics in ensuring the integrity and authenticity of preserved digital materials.
*   Develop a basic understanding of risk assessment and disaster recovery planning for digital archives.

#### Detailed lesson content
With the legal and ethical frameworks in place, we now turn to the technical "how-to" of long-term digital preservation. The digital world is characterized by rapid technological change, meaning that simply storing a file isn't enough; active strategies are required to keep it accessible. One of the most common preservation strategies is **migration**. This involves converting digital objects from an older, potentially obsolete format to a newer, more stable one. For example, migrating a WordPerfect document to a PDF/A or an older image format to TIFF. The challenge with migration is ensuring data fidelity – that no information is lost or altered during the conversion. This requires careful testing and validation. A related concept is **normalization**, where diverse file formats are converted into a single, standard preservation format within the archive, simplifying management. For instance, an archive might normalize all incoming word processing documents to XML or PDF/A, regardless of their original format.

An alternative, or complementary, strategy is **emulation**. Instead of changing the file format, emulation involves creating software that mimics the behavior of an older hardware or software environment. This allows the original digital object to be run in its native environment, preserving its original look, feel, and functionality. Emulation is particularly valuable for complex born-digital objects like interactive multimedia, video games, or software art, where the user experience is integral to the content. Imagine trying to preserve an early CD-ROM game; migration might lose its interactive elements, but emulation could allow it to run on a modern computer. While powerful, emulation requires significant technical expertise and resources to develop and maintain emulators for various platforms. A common mistake is to assume that migration is always the best solution; for complex, interactive works, emulation might be the only way to truly preserve the original experience.

Beyond these strategies, the physical location and management of digital data are critical. **Storage solutions** come in various forms. **Local storage** involves maintaining servers and storage devices directly within your institution. This offers maximum control over data security and access, but requires significant upfront investment in hardware, IT infrastructure, and personnel for maintenance, backups, and environmental controls. **Cloud storage**, on the other hand, involves storing data on remote servers managed by a third-party provider (e.g., Amazon S3, Google Cloud Storage, Microsoft Azure). Cloud solutions offer scalability, often lower upfront costs, and built-in redundancy, but raise concerns about data sovereignty, vendor lock-in, and the security practices of the provider. Many organizations adopt a hybrid approach, storing primary copies locally and using cloud for redundant backups, or vice versa. The "3-2-1 rule" is a good guideline: keep at least three copies of your data, on two different storage media, with one copy offsite.

Ensuring the integrity and authenticity of digital objects also involves techniques from **digital forensics**. This discipline, often associated with law enforcement, provides methods for acquiring, preserving, and analyzing digital evidence in a forensically sound manner. For digital preservation, forensic techniques are used to verify that a digital object has not been tampered with, to recover corrupted data, or to establish a chain of custody for an archived item. Tools like checksums (e.g., MD5, SHA-256) are fundamental here; they generate a unique digital fingerprint for a file. If even a single bit changes, the checksum will be different, indicating potential corruption or alteration. Regularly re-calculating and comparing checksums is a vital preservation action to monitor data integrity over time.

Finally, no preservation plan is complete without **risk assessment and disaster recovery planning**. Digital archives are vulnerable to a range of threats: hardware failure, software bugs, cyberattacks, natural disasters (fire, flood), and human error. A risk assessment identifies potential threats, evaluates their likelihood and impact, and prioritizes mitigation strategies. This might involve identifying single points of failure, assessing the vulnerability of storage systems, or evaluating the human element in data handling. **Disaster recovery planning** outlines the procedures for responding to and recovering from a catastrophic event. This includes having offsite backups, documented recovery procedures, and designated personnel responsible for executing the plan. For a DH project, this means thinking beyond the immediate project lifespan to consider what happens if the server crashes, the lead researcher leaves, or the funding runs out. Proactive planning is the cornerstone of sustainable digital preservation.

#### Key concepts
*   **Migration:** A digital preservation strategy that involves converting digital objects from an older, potentially obsolete format to a newer, more stable one to ensure continued accessibility.
*   **Normalization:** A specific type of migration where diverse file formats are converted into a single, standard preservation format within an archive.
*   **Emulation:** A digital preservation strategy that involves creating software that mimics the behavior of an older hardware or software environment, allowing original digital objects to run in their native state.
*   **Local Storage:** Storing digital data on servers and storage devices maintained directly by the owning institution or project.
*   **Cloud Storage:** Storing digital data on remote servers managed by a third-party provider over the internet.
*   **3-2-1 Rule:** A data backup strategy recommending at least three copies of data, on two different storage media, with one copy offsite.
*   **Digital Forensics:** The application of scientific methods to acquire, preserve, and analyze digital evidence in a forensically sound manner, used in preservation to verify integrity and authenticity.
*   **Checksum:** A small-sized datum derived from a block of digital data for the purpose of detecting errors that may have been introduced during its transmission or storage.
*   **Risk Assessment:** The process of identifying potential threats to a digital archive, evaluating their likelihood and impact, and prioritizing mitigation strategies.
*   **Disaster Recovery Planning:** The process of developing a systematic plan for responding to and recovering from catastrophic events that could affect a digital archive.

#### Hands-on activity
**Preservation Strategy Selection and Rationale**
You are working on a DH project that has created two distinct types of digital objects:
1.  A large collection of digitized 18th-century newspaper pages (high-resolution TIFF images and OCR text).
2.  A complex, interactive 3D model of a historical building, created using specialized architectural software, with embedded metadata and user-driven navigation.

For each type of digital object, propose the *most appropriate primary preservation strategy* (migration, emulation, or normalization) and explain your rationale in 200-300 words. Consider the unique characteristics of each object and the challenges each strategy addresses.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Multiple Choice Question:**
    A DH project has created a highly interactive, Flash-based multimedia exhibit that integrates video, audio, and user-driven navigation. Which digital preservation strategy would be *most effective* for preserving the original user experience and functionality of this exhibit?
    a) Migration to a standard HTML5 format.
    b) Normalization of all embedded media files to WAV and JPEG.
    c) Emulation of the original Flash environment.
    d) Simply backing up the Flash files to cloud storage.

    **Correct Answer:** c) Emulation of the original Flash environment. (Migration might lose interactivity, normalization is a type of migration, and backup alone doesn't address obsolescence.)

2.  **Short Essay Question:**
    Explain the purpose of using checksums in digital preservation and how they contribute to ensuring the "integrity" of digital objects. Provide a practical example of how checksums would be used in the ongoing management of a digital archive.

    **Model Response Rubric:**
    *   **Excellent (5 points):** Clearly defines checksums and explains their role in detecting any changes (corruption, tampering) to a digital file. Connects this directly to the concept of "integrity" (ensuring completeness and uncorrupted state). Provides a concrete, practical example of checksum use in an archive, such as calculating checksums upon ingest, storing them in metadata, and periodically re-calculating and comparing them to detect bit rot or accidental changes.
    *   **Good (3-4 points):** Defines checksums and generally explains their purpose in detecting changes. Mentions integrity but might not fully elaborate the connection. Provides a somewhat general example of their use.
    *   **Fair (1-2 points):** Provides a vague definition or explanation of checksums. Misses the connection to integrity. Example is generic or missing.
    *   **Needs Improvement (0 points):** Does not address the prompt or provides incorrect information.

#### AI generation note
Design a 12-minute interactive simulation and video lecture. Start with a 5-minute animated sequence demonstrating migration (e.g., converting a legacy word processor file to PDF/A, showing potential data loss/gain) and emulation (e.g., running an old game in an emulator, highlighting preserved functionality). Then, present a "storage dilemma" interactive scenario where learners choose between local and cloud storage for a DH project, with pop-up explanations of the pros and cons for each choice (cost, security, control). Dedicate 3 minutes to a visual explanation of checksums, showing how a single bit change alters the checksum. Conclude with a "Disaster Recovery Checklist" interactive element where learners identify critical steps. Provide full captions and a downloadable comparison chart of preservation strategies.

### Chapter 5.6 — Designing and Managing a DH Archiving Project

#### Learning objectives
*   Outline the key phases of a digital archiving project lifecycle within a DH context, from planning to sustainability.
*   Identify crucial stakeholders in a DH archiving project and strategies for effective engagement.
*   Evaluate various tools and workflows for the ingest, processing, and dissemination of digital archival materials.
*   Develop a sustainability plan for a DH archiving project, considering funding, technical infrastructure, and long-term commitment.

#### Detailed lesson content
We've explored the theoretical underpinnings, technical considerations, and ethical complexities of digital archiving. Now, we bring it all together by focusing on the practicalities of designing and managing a Digital Humanities archiving project. This is where theory meets practice, and where careful planning can make the difference between a fleeting digital experiment and an enduring scholarly resource. A DH archiving project, much like any large-scale DH endeavor, follows a lifecycle that typically includes: **planning and scoping**, where you define the project's goals, identify the collection, assess resources, and conduct initial risk assessments; **selection and appraisal**, deciding what to preserve and to what extent; **ingest**, the process of transferring digital objects into the archive system, often involving format validation and metadata creation; **preservation actions**, applying strategies like migration or emulation; **storage and security**, ensuring data integrity and redundancy; **access and dissemination**, making the archive available to its designated community; and crucially, **sustainability and long-term management**, ensuring the project's viability beyond its initial funding cycle. This lifecycle is iterative, meaning that steps may be revisited as the project evolves or new challenges emerge.

Effective project management in DH archiving hinges on robust **stakeholder engagement**. Digital archives are rarely built in isolation. Key stakeholders typically include: the **creators/producers** of the digital content (e.g., researchers, artists, community members); the **archive staff/curators** who manage the repository; **IT professionals** who maintain the infrastructure; **funders** who provide financial support; the **designated community/users** who will access the archive; and potentially **legal experts** for copyright and privacy. Engaging these diverse groups from the outset is vital for success. For example, involving community members in the selection and description of their cultural heritage materials can ensure culturally appropriate representation and build trust. Collaborating with IT staff early on can prevent technical bottlenecks and ensure the chosen repository system aligns with institutional infrastructure. A common mistake is to view archiving as a purely technical task, neglecting the human and institutional dimensions, leading to misaligned expectations or unsustainable solutions.

The practical execution of an archiving project relies on well-defined **tools and workflows** for ingest, processing, and dissemination. The **ingest** phase involves getting the digital objects into the repository. This might use automated scripts for bulk uploads, manual metadata entry forms, or specialized tools for format validation (e.g., DROID for identifying file formats, JHOVE for validating them). **Processing** involves applying preservation actions, enriching metadata, and creating derivatives (e.g., lower-resolution images for web display from preservation masters). Tools like OpenRefine can be invaluable for cleaning and standardizing metadata. For **dissemination**, the repository system itself often provides the access interface, but projects might also leverage web frameworks (like Omeka for exhibitions) or APIs to allow researchers to programmatically access data for analysis. The choice of tools should align with the project's scale, the technical expertise of the team, and the specific characteristics of the digital objects. For instance, a small project with limited technical staff might opt for a simpler, more out-of-the-box solution, while a large-scale institutional archive might invest in custom-scripted workflows and enterprise-level software.

Perhaps the most challenging aspect of DH archiving is ensuring **sustainability**. Many DH projects are grant-funded, and the question of what happens when the grant ends is perennial. A robust sustainability plan addresses:
1.  **Funding:** How will ongoing maintenance, software upgrades, and staff salaries be covered? This might involve institutional commitment, endowment funds, or diversified funding models.
2.  **Technical Infrastructure:** How will hardware be refreshed, software updated, and data migrated over decades? This requires clear agreements with institutional IT or trusted third-party preservation services.
3.  **Staffing and Expertise:** Who will maintain the archive? What training is needed? Succession planning is crucial.
4.  **Community and Governance:** How will the archive remain relevant to its designated community? What governance structures are in place for decision-making?
A practical example of a sustainability challenge is a project that builds a custom database and website, only to find that the original developers have moved on, and no one else has the expertise to maintain it. This underscores the importance of using open standards, well-documented code, and planning for knowledge transfer. Successful DH archiving projects are not just technologically sound; they are institutionally supported, financially viable, and community-engaged for the long haul.

#### Key concepts
*   **Project Lifecycle (Archiving):** The systematic stages of a digital archiving project, typically including planning, selection, ingest, preservation actions, storage, access, and sustainability.
*   **Stakeholder Engagement:** The process of involving individuals or groups who have an interest in or are affected by a project, crucial for successful DH archiving.
*   **Ingest:** The process of transferring digital objects and their associated metadata into a digital archive or repository.
*   **Processing:** The actions taken on digital objects after ingest, such as applying preservation strategies, enriching metadata, or creating derivatives.
*   **Dissemination:** The act of making digital archival materials available and accessible to the designated community.
*   **Sustainability Plan:** A strategic document outlining how a digital archiving project will maintain its operations, technical infrastructure, funding, and relevance over the long term.
*   **DRUID (Digital Repository of the University of Illinois at Urbana-Champaign):** An example of a large-scale institutional repository that demonstrates comprehensive archiving project management. (Note: While not a specific tool, it represents a successful implementation of archiving principles and project management.)
*   **OpenRefine:** A powerful tool for cleaning, transforming, and extending data, often used in the processing phase of DH archiving projects.

#### Hands-on activity
**Developing a Sustainability Statement**
You have successfully completed a DH project that created a digital archive of early 20th-century urban planning documents, including maps, photographs, and textual reports. The project was funded by a 3-year grant. Now, you need to write a sustainability statement (300-400 words) to secure institutional commitment for its long-term preservation and access.

Your statement should address:
1.  The value proposition of the archive for the institution and its designated community.
2.  Proposed strategies for ongoing funding (e.g., institutional budget lines, endowment, future grants).
3.  Plans for technical infrastructure maintenance (e.g., integration with existing IT, software updates, hardware refresh).
4.  Strategies for staffing and expertise retention (e.g., training, designated roles).
5.  How community engagement and access will be maintained.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Multiple Choice Question:**
    In the context of designing and managing a DH archiving project, which of the following is *least likely* to be considered a primary stakeholder?
    a) The researchers who created the digital content.
    b) The IT department responsible for server maintenance.
    c) A random member of the general public with no direct connection to the archive's subject matter.
    d) The funding agency that provided the initial grant.

    **Correct Answer:** c) A random member of the general public with no direct connection to the archive's subject matter. (While public access is a goal, a "random member" is not typically a *primary* stakeholder in the project design and management phase, unlike the other options.)

2.  **Short Essay Question:**
    You are designing a workflow for the ingest phase of a DH project digitizing a large collection of rare books. Describe at least three distinct steps in this ingest workflow, explaining the purpose of each step and mentioning any specific tools or considerations relevant to ensuring long-term preservation.

    **Model Response Rubric:**
    *   **Excellent (5 points):** Describes three distinct, logical steps in the ingest workflow (e.g., file transfer/acquisition, format validation, metadata creation/association, checksum generation). For each step, clearly explains its purpose in the context of preservation (e.g., ensuring files are complete, in acceptable formats, discoverable, verifiable). Mentions specific tools or considerations (e.g., secure transfer protocols, DROID/JHOVE for validation, Dublin Core/MODS for metadata, MD5/SHA-256 for checksums).
    *   **Good (3-4 points):** Describes three steps, but they might be less distinct or detailed. Explains the purpose generally. Mentions some tools or considerations but might lack specificity.
    *   **Fair (1-2 points):** Provides a list of steps without clear explanation of purpose or tools.
    *   **Needs Improvement (0 points):** Does not address the prompt or provides incorrect information.

#### AI generation note
Create a 15-minute video lecture with a "project management dashboard" visual style. Start with an animated timeline illustrating the phases of a DH archiving project lifecycle, with pop-up text for key activities in each phase. Dedicate 5 minutes to a "stakeholder map" animation, showing different stakeholder groups (creators, IT, funders, community) and their interconnections, using a specific DH project (e.g., a community-based oral history archive) as an example. Then, conduct a 4-minute "tools and workflows" segment, demonstrating (via screen capture or animation) how tools like DROID for format identification, OpenRefine for metadata cleaning, and a repository's ingest interface might be used. Conclude with a "Sustainability Strategy Builder" interactive exercise where learners select and justify elements for a long-term plan. Provide full captions and a downloadable project lifecycle template.
