### Chapter 8.6 — Evaluation, Assessment, and Impact

#### Learning objectives
*   Develop a framework for evaluating the scholarly merit and technical robustness of Digital Humanities projects.
*   Identify various metrics and methods for assessing user experience and project usability.
*   Articulate the potential academic, public, and pedagogical impacts of DH initiatives.
*   Design strategies for gathering and incorporating feedback throughout a project's lifecycle.
*   Understand the challenges and opportunities in demonstrating the value of DH work to diverse stakeholders.

#### Detailed lesson content
The completion of a Digital Humanities project is not the end of the journey; it marks the beginning of its **evaluation, assessment, and the articulation of its impact**. Unlike traditional humanities outputs like monographs or articles, which have established peer review mechanisms, DH projects often require a multi-faceted approach to evaluation. This involves assessing not only their scholarly rigor and contribution to knowledge but also their technical functionality, usability, and broader societal impact. A common pitfall for DH practitioners is to focus solely on the technical achievement, neglecting to systematically evaluate the project's effectiveness in addressing its research questions or engaging its intended audience.

Evaluating the **scholarly merit** of a DH project involves several criteria. Does the project genuinely advance a humanities argument or open new avenues of inquiry? Does it employ appropriate methodologies and interpret its findings rigorously within a humanistic framework? For instance, a network analysis of intellectual correspondence should not just present a pretty graph, but critically interpret the network's structure, its central figures, and its implications for understanding knowledge dissemination, drawing on historical context and existing scholarship. Peer review, adapted for digital outputs, is increasingly common, with journals and platforms like *Digital Humanities Quarterly* or *Journal of Digital Humanities* offering venues for publishing and reviewing digital scholarship. This often involves assessing the project's documentation, code, data, and the scholarly argument it embodies.

Beyond scholarly merit, **technical robustness and usability** are paramount. A brilliant scholarly argument embedded in a buggy, slow, or unintuitive interface will struggle to find an audience. Technical evaluation assesses the project's code quality, data integrity, scalability, and long-term maintainability. Is the code well-documented? Is the data clean and well-structured? Can the system handle increased traffic or larger datasets? Usability assessment focuses on the user experience (UX). Can users easily navigate the interface? Is the information presented clearly? Are interactive features intuitive? Methods for assessing usability include **heuristic evaluations** (expert review against established usability principles), **think-aloud protocols** (users verbalize their thoughts while interacting with the system), **surveys**, and **analytics tracking** (monitoring user behavior on the site, though with privacy considerations). Feedback gathered through these methods is crucial for iterative refinement, ensuring the project is not just functional but also effective and enjoyable to use.

The **impact** of a DH project extends beyond traditional academic metrics. It can be categorized into several areas:
1.  **Academic Impact:** Contribution to scholarly discourse, new research questions generated, publications, citations, and integration into teaching. For example, a digital edition might enable new forms of textual analysis or provide a foundation for subsequent scholarly work.
2.  **Public Impact:** Engagement with broader audiences, public history initiatives, educational resources for K-12 or lifelong learners, cultural heritage preservation, and fostering digital literacy. A project mapping historical neighborhoods might empower local communities to explore their heritage.
3.  **Pedagogical Impact:** Use in classrooms, development of new teaching methods, training students in digital skills, and fostering critical thinking about digital tools and data. A visualization tool for literary themes could transform how students engage with classic texts.

Demonstrating this multifaceted impact requires a strategic approach. It involves documenting user engagement (e.g., website analytics, workshop attendance), collecting testimonials, tracking citations and mentions in popular media, and clearly articulating the project's contributions in grant applications and scholarly reports. The challenge lies in moving beyond simply describing the project to actively *measuring* and *communicating* its value to diverse stakeholders—funders, academic peers, university administrators, and the general public. This often requires a narrative that translates technical achievements and scholarly insights into compelling stories about knowledge creation, cultural preservation, and public engagement.

#### Key concepts
*   **Evaluation:** The systematic process of assessing the merit, worth, or value of a DH project, often considering scholarly rigor, technical robustness, and user experience.
*   **Usability:** The ease with which users can learn to operate, prepare inputs for, and interpret outputs of a system or program. It's a key component of user experience (UX).
*   **Heuristic Evaluation:** A usability inspection method where expert evaluators assess a user interface against a set of established usability principles (heuristics).
*   **Think-Aloud Protocol:** A method used in user testing where participants verbalize their thoughts, feelings, and actions while interacting with a system.
*   **Academic Impact:** The contribution of a project to scholarly knowledge, research, and teaching within the academic community.
*   **Public Impact:** The broader societal benefits and engagement generated by a project, reaching audiences beyond academia.
*   **Pedagogical Impact:** The influence of a project on teaching, learning, and curriculum development.

#### Hands-on activity
**Developing an Evaluation Plan**

You have developed an interactive online exhibit titled "Mapping Literary London," which visualizes the locations mentioned in 19th-century novels and allows users to explore character movements and thematic connections.

**Your Task:**
1.  **Propose two distinct methods for evaluating the scholarly merit** of this project. How would you measure its contribution to literary studies or historical geography?
2.  **Suggest two methods for assessing the usability and user experience** of the interactive map. What specific aspects would you focus on?
3.  **Describe one specific way you would measure the public impact** of this exhibit.

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** A DH project has created a searchable database of historical recipes, complete with ingredient analysis and cultural context. The project team is preparing a report for their university's research office. Which of the following would be the *least* effective way to demonstrate the project's academic impact?
    a) Citing peer-reviewed articles that have used the database as a primary source.
    b) Documenting how the database has been integrated into university courses for teaching food history.
    c) Reporting the number of unique visitors to the website each month.
    d) Publishing a scholarly article that analyzes patterns revealed by the database.

    **Model Response:**
    The least effective way to demonstrate academic impact is **c) Reporting the number of unique visitors to the website each month.**
    *   **Why (c) is least effective:** While website visitor numbers can indicate public interest or reach, they do not inherently measure academic impact. A high number of visitors doesn't necessarily mean the project is advancing scholarly knowledge or being used by researchers. It's a metric of public engagement, not academic rigor or contribution.
    *   **Why others are effective:**
        *   (a) Citing peer-reviewed articles directly demonstrates that other scholars are using the project for their research, a strong indicator of academic utility and impact.
        *   (b) Integration into university courses shows pedagogical impact, which is a significant component of academic impact, demonstrating its value for teaching and training future scholars.
        *   (d) Publishing a scholarly article based on the database's findings directly contributes new knowledge to the field, which is the core of academic impact.

2.  **Question:** You are evaluating the usability of a new DH tool designed to help scholars perform sentiment analysis on historical texts. You notice that many users struggle to correctly upload their text files and configure the analysis parameters, despite clear instructions. What specific usability assessment method would be most effective for diagnosing these problems, and what kind of feedback would you expect to gain?

    **Model Response:**
    The most effective usability assessment method for diagnosing these specific problems (struggling with file upload and parameter configuration despite instructions) would be a **Think-Aloud Protocol** combined with **Task-Based User Testing**.

    *   **Method Description:** I would recruit a small group of target users (e.g., historians or literary scholars) and ask them to perform specific tasks, such as "Upload a text file and run a sentiment analysis on it, configuring the lexicon for 18th-century English." While they perform these tasks, I would instruct them to verbalize *everything* they are thinking, feeling, and doing. I would observe their interactions closely, noting where they hesitate, make errors, or express confusion.

    *   **Expected Feedback:**
        *   **Specific Pain Points:** I would gain precise insights into *where* users are getting stuck (e.g., "I don't see a clear 'upload' button," "The file format requirements are unclear," "I don't understand what 'lexicon configuration' means").
        *   **Cognitive Load:** I would understand the mental models users are bringing to the tool and where the tool's design deviates from those expectations, causing cognitive friction.
        *   **Effectiveness of Instructions:** I would see if the "clear instructions" are actually being read, understood, or even found by users. Often, instructions are clear to the designer but not to the user.
        *   **Unexpected Workarounds:** Users might try unexpected ways to achieve tasks, revealing alternative mental paths that the design doesn't support.
        *   **Feature Prioritization:** Feedback might reveal that certain configuration parameters are confusing or unnecessary for initial use, suggesting a need for simplification or progressive disclosure.

    This method provides rich qualitative data that goes beyond simply knowing *that* users are struggling, helping to understand *why* they are struggling.

#### AI generation note
Create a 13-minute video lecture. Start with an instructor outlining a multi-faceted evaluation framework (scholarly, technical, usability, impact). Use a split-screen to show examples of scholarly outputs (e.g., a DH journal article) alongside technical documentation (e.g., GitHub README) and a simple usability survey. Include a 4-minute animated segment demonstrating different usability testing methods (e.g., a user speaking their thoughts while interacting with a mock-up, an eye-tracking visualization). Conclude with a segment on articulating impact, featuring short quotes from project stakeholders (e.g., a student, a museum curator, a funder) describing the value of a hypothetical DH project. Include a downloadable template for a project evaluation rubric. Provide captions and a transcript.

---

### Chapter 8.7 — Dissemination, Publishing, and Sustainability

#### Learning objectives
*   Identify various platforms and strategies for disseminating Digital Humanities projects to academic and public audiences.
*   Understand the principles of open access and open scholarship in the context of DH publishing.
*   Develop a plan for the long-term preservation and sustainability of digital research outputs.
*   Explore funding models and grant writing strategies for sustaining DH projects beyond initial development.
*   Articulate the importance of documentation and metadata for project reusability and longevity.

#### Detailed lesson content
The ultimate goal of most Digital Humanities projects is not merely to exist, but to be discovered, used, and sustained over time. Thus, **dissemination, publishing, and sustainability** are not afterthoughts but integral components of the project lifecycle. A groundbreaking digital project that remains undiscovered or becomes inaccessible after a few years represents a significant loss of scholarly effort and potential impact. This phase requires strategic planning, often beginning even before development is complete, to ensure the project reaches its intended audience and remains viable for the long term.

**Dissemination strategies** must be tailored to the project's audience and goals. For academic audiences, traditional scholarly communication venues remain important, but adapted for digital work. This includes publishing articles *about* the digital project in peer-reviewed journals (e.g., *Digital Humanities Quarterly*, *Journal of Digital Humanities*), presenting at DH conferences (e.g., DH conference, MLA, AHA), and submitting the project itself for review by digital humanities specific peer-review platforms. For public audiences, strategies might include creating a user-friendly website, engaging through social media, collaborating with museums or cultural institutions, developing educational modules, or producing documentary videos. Platforms like **Omeka S** (for creating digital collections and exhibits), **Scalar** (for networked, media-rich scholarship), or **Manifold Digital Publishing** (for interactive monographs) offer robust frameworks for publishing and presenting DH work. The choice of platform often dictates the project's discoverability and its capacity for rich, interactive presentation.

A core principle guiding contemporary DH dissemination is **open access and open scholarship**. This means making research outputs—including data, code, and publications—freely available online, without paywalls or restrictive licenses. Adhering to open access principles maximizes the project's reach, fosters collaboration, and accelerates knowledge creation. Creative Commons licenses (e.g., CC BY, CC BY-NC) are commonly used to specify how others can use and build upon the project's components. The commitment to open scholarship extends to making the underlying data and code publicly available, often in repositories like GitHub or institutional data archives, allowing other scholars to verify findings, reuse data, and build new tools. This transparency is a hallmark of rigorous digital scholarship.

**Long-term preservation and sustainability** are perhaps the most challenging aspects of DH project management. Digital objects are inherently fragile; file formats become obsolete, hardware degrades, and software dependencies break. A project needs a **preservation plan** from its inception. This involves:
1.  **Archiving Data:** Storing raw and processed data in stable, well-maintained institutional repositories (e.g., university libraries, national archives) that adhere to preservation standards.
2.  **Archiving Code:** Placing source code in version-controlled repositories (e.g., GitHub, GitLab) with clear documentation and dependencies.
3.  **Archiving the Project Output:** This is the trickiest. For dynamic, interactive projects, simply archiving static files may not capture the full user experience. Strategies include creating "emulations" (running old software on new systems), "migrations" (converting to new formats), or "capturing" the interactive experience through video documentation.
4.  **Documentation:** Comprehensive documentation of the project's methodology, data models, code, and technical environment is crucial for future researchers to understand, reuse, and potentially re-implement the project.

**Funding and grant writing** are often essential for long-term sustainability. Initial project funding might cover development, but ongoing maintenance, server costs, software updates, and potential migrations require continuous support. Grant proposals for DH projects need to clearly articulate the scholarly significance, the innovative digital methodologies, the project's impact, and a detailed sustainability plan. Funders are increasingly interested in how projects will remain accessible and useful beyond the grant period. This requires demonstrating a commitment to open standards, robust documentation, and partnerships with institutions (like libraries or archives) that have a mandate for long-term digital preservation. The project's longevity often depends on integrating it into an institutional infrastructure that can provide ongoing support, rather than relying solely on individual researchers or short-term grants.

#### Key concepts
*   **Dissemination:** The act of spreading or making widely known the results, findings, or outputs of a research project to its intended audiences.
*   **Open Access:** The practice of providing unrestricted online access to peer-reviewed scholarly research, including publications, data, and code.
*   **Open Scholarship:** A broader movement encompassing open access, open data, open educational resources, and open methodologies, promoting transparency and collaboration in research.
*   **Sustainability Plan:** A strategic document outlining how a digital project will be maintained, updated, and remain accessible and functional over the long term, often including funding, technical, and institutional support strategies.
*   **Digital Preservation:** The set of activities required to ensure that digital objects remain accessible and usable over time, despite technological changes and media degradation.
*   **Creative Commons Licenses:** Public copyright licenses that enable the free distribution of an otherwise copyrighted work, allowing creators to communicate which rights they reserve, and which rights they waive for the benefit of recipients or other creators.

#### Hands-on activity
**Developing a Sustainability Statement**

You have just completed a successful DH project: "The Digital Atlas of Early Modern Travel Narratives," an interactive map and database of voyages, routes, and cultural encounters. You are now applying for a grant to fund its ongoing maintenance and expansion.

**Your Task:**
1.  **Draft a short "Sustainability Statement" (approx. 150-200 words)** for your grant application.
2.  In this statement, address the following:
    *   How will the project's data and code be preserved?
    *   What institutional partnerships will support its long-term viability?
    *   How will ongoing maintenance (e.g., server costs, software updates) be addressed?
    *   What is the plan for keeping the project accessible and usable for future generations of scholars and the public?

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** A DH project has developed an innovative interactive visualization tool for analyzing historical demographic data. The team wants to ensure its findings and the tool itself are widely accessible and reusable by other scholars. Which two actions would best align with the principles of open scholarship?
    a) Publish a paywalled article describing the tool and host the tool on a private server.
    b) Apply a Creative Commons CC BY license to the tool's code and data, and publish an open access article about it.
c) Present the tool at an exclusive academic conference and only share the code with collaborators.
d) Copyright all data and code, and only make the tool accessible via a subscription model.

    **Model Response:**
    The two actions that best align with the principles of open scholarship are **b) Apply a Creative Commons CC BY license to the tool's code and data, and publish an open access article about it.**
    *   **Why (b) is best:**
        *   **Creative Commons CC BY license:** This license allows others to use, adapt, and build upon the code and data, even commercially, as long as they give attribution. This directly supports reusability and interoperability.
        *   **Open access article:** Publishing the article open access ensures that the scholarly findings and the description of the tool are freely available to anyone, maximizing dissemination and academic impact without financial barriers.
    *   **Why others are less suitable:**
        *   (a) A paywalled article and private server restrict access and reuse, directly contradicting open scholarship.
        *   (c) Exclusive conference presentations and limited code sharing hinder broad dissemination and collaboration.
        *   (d) Copyrighting all data/code and using a subscription model severely limits accessibility and reuse.

2.  **Question:** Explain the concept of "digital rot" in the context of Digital Humanities projects and describe two specific strategies for mitigating its effects on a web-based interactive exhibit.

    **Model Response:**
    **"Digital rot"** (also known as bit rot or data decay) refers to the gradual degradation or obsolescence of digital information and the technologies used to access it. It's not physical decay but rather the loss of functionality or accessibility over time due to factors like:
    *   **File format obsolescence:** Older file formats (e.g., WordStar, early multimedia formats) become unreadable by modern software.
    *   **Software dependency issues:** Interactive projects rely on specific programming languages, libraries, and operating systems that evolve or become unsupported.
    *   **Hardware degradation:** Storage media (hard drives, CDs) can fail or become unreadable.
    *   **Link rot:** URLs break, making external resources inaccessible.
    *   **Lack of documentation:** Without clear documentation, future users cannot understand or re-implement the project.

    Two specific strategies for mitigating the effects of digital rot on a web-based interactive exhibit are:
    1.  **Emulation and Migration Planning:**
        *   **Emulation:** This involves creating software that mimics the behavior of older hardware and software environments, allowing the original exhibit to run in its native environment on modern systems. This is particularly useful for highly interactive or complex exhibits where the specific user experience is crucial.
        *   **Migration:** This involves converting the exhibit's data and code to newer, more stable formats and platforms as technology evolves. For example, migrating from an older JavaScript library to a more current one, or converting proprietary database formats to open standards like SQL. A comprehensive plan would involve periodic assessment of technologies and scheduled migrations.
    2.  **Robust Documentation and Archiving in Trusted Repositories:**
        *   **Comprehensive Documentation:** Create detailed documentation covering the project's technical architecture, data models, code dependencies, installation instructions, and user manual. This "read-me" file should be meticulously maintained and updated.
        *   **Archiving:** Deposit all project components (source code, raw data, processed data, static versions of the exhibit, and documentation) in a trusted digital repository (e.g., a university library's institutional repository, Zenodo, CLOCKSS). These repositories are designed for long-term preservation, employing strategies like redundant storage, format normalization, and checksum verification to ensure data integrity and accessibility for future generations.

#### AI generation note
Create a 15-minute documentary-style video. Start with archival footage of outdated technology (e.g., floppy disks, early web pages) to illustrate "digital rot." Then, transition to an instructor explaining dissemination strategies, showcasing screenshots of successful DH projects on platforms like Omeka, Scalar, and Manifold. Dedicate a 5-minute segment to open access, using a visual metaphor of an open gate vs. a locked door for research. The final 5 minutes should focus on sustainability, featuring an interview clip with a digital archivist discussing preservation challenges and solutions (e.g., institutional partnerships, migration strategies). Include a 2-question interactive quiz on Creative Commons licenses. Provide full captions and a downloadable checklist for a DH project sustainability plan.

---

### Chapter 8.8 — Capstone Project Workshop: Synthesis and Presentation

#### Learning objectives
*   Synthesize knowledge and skills acquired throughout the MicroMasters program into a coherent capstone project proposal.
*   Develop a compelling narrative for presenting a DH project, effectively communicating its scholarly significance and digital methodology.
*   Refine project proposals based on peer feedback and critical self-assessment.
*   Design effective visual aids and presentation materials for communicating complex DH concepts.
*   Articulate the potential challenges and ethical considerations of their proposed capstone project.

#### Detailed lesson content
This final chapter serves as a capstone workshop, bringing together all the theoretical knowledge and practical skills acquired throughout the Digital Humanities MicroMasters. The goal is to guide learners in synthesizing their understanding into a robust and compelling capstone project proposal, preparing them for the culmination of their studies. This isn't just about presenting an idea; it's about articulating a well-researched, methodologically sound, and practically feasible DH project that demonstrates mastery of the course material. The emphasis here is on **synthesis, critical self-assessment, and effective scholarly communication**.

A strong capstone project proposal begins with a clear and concise **project abstract** that immediately conveys the project's core research question, its primary methodology, and its anticipated contribution. Following this, the proposal must elaborate on the **scholarly context**, demonstrating how the project engages with existing humanities scholarship and identifies a gap that DH methods can uniquely fill. This requires a mini-literature review, showing an awareness of both traditional and digital scholarship in the chosen field. For example, if proposing a network analysis of Renaissance patronage, the proposal would need to reference key historians of the Renaissance and relevant DH projects that have explored similar social structures. A common mistake is to describe the project in isolation, failing to situate it within broader academic conversations.

The heart of the proposal lies in the detailed articulation of the **digital methodology and tools**. This section moves beyond simply naming tools to explaining *why* specific methods (e.g., topic modeling, GIS, network analysis) are appropriate for answering the research question, and *how* the chosen tools will be used. It requires a discussion of the data sources, including their provenance, potential biases, and the plan for data acquisition and curation (e.g., "We will use the digitized correspondence from the X archive, applying OCR and then manually correcting errors for named entities, which will then be encoded in TEI XML to facilitate network extraction"). Learners should also address the anticipated challenges in data handling or tool implementation and propose realistic solutions. This demonstrates a critical understanding of both the potential and limitations of digital approaches.

Effective **presentation and communication** are paramount for a capstone project. A brilliant idea poorly communicated will fail to impress. This involves developing a clear narrative arc for the presentation, starting with the problem, moving to the proposed solution (the DH project), detailing the methodology, and concluding with the anticipated impact and future directions. Visual aids, such as mock-ups of interfaces, sample data visualizations, or flowcharts of the project workflow, are essential for illustrating complex digital components. These visuals should be clear, uncluttered, and directly support the spoken argument. Practicing the presentation, receiving peer feedback, and refining both content and delivery are crucial steps. The tone should be confident, scholarly, and enthusiastic, conveying genuine passion for the research.

Finally, the capstone proposal must address **ethical considerations and a preliminary sustainability plan**. Building on previous modules, learners should identify potential ethical issues related to their data (e.g., privacy, intellectual property, representational bias) and outline how they plan to mitigate these. A brief sustainability statement, even for a hypothetical project, demonstrates foresight regarding long-term preservation, maintenance, and potential future funding. This holistic approach to project design, from conceptualization to ethical considerations and sustainability, signifies a comprehensive understanding of the DH project lifecycle. The capstone workshop provides a structured environment for learners to refine these elements, receive constructive criticism, and ultimately craft a compelling vision for their own contribution to the Digital Humanities.

#### Key concepts
*   **Capstone Project:** A culminating academic experience that requires students to synthesize and apply knowledge and skills acquired throughout their program of study to a significant project.
*   **Project Proposal:** A formal document outlining a proposed project, including its objectives, methodology, timeline, resources, and anticipated outcomes.
*   **Scholarly Context:** The existing body of academic research and discourse within which a new project is situated, demonstrating its relevance and contribution to the field.
*   **Digital Methodology:** The specific digital methods and computational approaches employed in a DH project to address its research questions.
*   **Peer Feedback:** Constructive criticism and suggestions provided by peers on a project proposal or presentation, crucial for refinement and improvement.
*   **Synthesis:** The combination of ideas to form a theory or system, in this context, bringing together various DH concepts and skills into a coherent project.

#### Hands-on activity
**Peer Review of Capstone Project Abstracts**

Prepare a 250-word abstract for your hypothetical capstone project. This abstract should clearly state:
1.  Your core research question.
2.  The primary DH methodology and tools you plan to use.
3.  The type of data you will work with.
4.  The anticipated scholarly contribution or output.

**Your Task:**
1.  **Write your abstract.**
2.  **Exchange abstracts with a peer (or imagine doing so).**
3.  **Provide feedback on your peer's abstract (and receive feedback on yours) using the following questions:**
    *   Is the research question clear, concise, and suitable for a DH approach?
    *   Are the methodology and tools clearly articulated and appropriate for the question?
    *   Is the anticipated contribution compelling and well-defined?
    *   Is the abstract engaging and easy to understand for a general DH audience?
    *   What is one specific suggestion for improvement?

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** A student proposes a capstone project to create a "digital archive of all medieval illuminated manuscripts." What is the most significant flaw in this proposal from a project design perspective, and how would you advise the student to revise it?

    **Model Response:**
    The most significant flaw in this proposal is its **unrealistic scope and lack of specificity**. "All medieval illuminated manuscripts" represents an astronomically vast and diverse corpus spanning centuries and continents, making the project practically impossible for a single capstone. It also lacks a specific research question that would drive the archive's design beyond mere aggregation.

    I would advise the student to revise the proposal by:
    *   **Narrowing the Scope:** Focus on a manageable subset. For example, "A digital archive of 13th-century illuminated manuscripts from the British Library focusing on depictions of daily life," or "A comparative study of marginalia in 15th-century Flemish Books of Hours." This makes the project feasible within a capstone timeframe and resource constraints.
    *   **Formulating a Specific Research Question:** Instead of just "creating an archive," the student needs an inquiry that the archive will help answer. For example, "How do the visual motifs in 13th-century British Library manuscripts reflect changing religious practices?" This question will guide data selection, metadata creation, and potential analytical tools.
    *   **Defining Methodology and Tools:** With a narrower scope and clear question, the student can then specify *how* they will digitize (if necessary), *how* they will describe images (e.g., using specific metadata schemas or image annotation tools), and *what* analytical tools (e.g., image analysis, visualization) they might employ to address their research question.

2.  **Question:** During a capstone project presentation, a student describes an innovative tool they built but spends very little time explaining the humanities research question it addresses or the scholarly significance of its findings. What critical element of DH project communication is missing, and why is it essential?

    **Model Response:**
    The critical element of DH project communication that is missing is the **integration of the "humanities" aspect with the "digital" aspect, specifically articulating the project's scholarly significance and its contribution to humanistic inquiry.**

    **Why it is essential:**
    *   **Justification of Digital Methods:** In Digital Humanities, tools and methods are not ends in themselves. They are means to an end: to answer humanities research questions, generate new insights, or preserve cultural heritage in novel ways. Without clearly explaining the scholarly problem and the insights gained, the digital tool becomes a mere technical exercise rather than a contribution to the humanities.
    *   **Audience Engagement:** A DH audience (which often includes both humanists and technologists) needs to understand *why* the project matters from a scholarly perspective. Humanists want to see the argument, the interpretation, and the new knowledge generated. Technologists want to understand the problem the tool solves.
    *   **Distinction from Pure Computer Science:** Without a strong humanistic argument, a DH project risks being perceived as a computer science project that happens to use humanities data, rather than a genuine contribution to the humanities discipline that leverages digital methods.
    *   **Impact and Value:** The scholarly significance is what ultimately defines the project's academic impact and value. It explains *why* anyone should care about the tool or its outputs beyond its technical elegance. It connects the project to the broader academic conversation and demonstrates its intellectual contribution.

#### AI generation note
Create a 15-minute interactive virtual workshop. Start with an instructor providing a checklist for a strong capstone proposal. Then, present a "mock" capstone proposal abstract (text on screen) and have the instructor provide real-time critical feedback, highlighting strengths and areas for improvement. Include a 5-minute segment where an instructor demonstrates effective use of visual aids for a DH presentation (e.g., showing a mock-up of a visualization, a data model diagram, and a project workflow chart, explaining *why* each visual is effective). Conclude with a guided reflection prompt asking learners to identify one area of their own capstone proposal that needs further refinement. Provide a downloadable capstone proposal template and a peer review rubric.
